Cardinal Characteristics on Bounded Generalised Baire Spaces
Tristan van der Vlugt

Abstract
We will give an overview of four families of cardinal characteristics defined on subspaces ∏ α ∈ κ b ⁡ ( α ) \prod_{\alpha\in\kappa}b(\alpha) of the generalised Baire space κ κ {}^{\kappa}\kappa , where κ \kappa is strongly inaccessible and b ∈ κ κ b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa . The considered families are bounded versions of the dominating, eventual difference, localisation and antilocalisation numbers, and their dual cardinals. We investigate parameters for which these cardinals are nontrivial and how the cardinals relate to each other and to other cardinals of the generalised Cichoń diagram. Finally we prove that different choices of parameters may lead to consistently distinct cardinals.



1. Introduction

Cardinal characteristics of the continuum are cardinal numbers that consistently lie strictly between
\aleph_{1}
and the cardinality of the reals
2^{\aleph_{0}}
. Many natural questions about the cardinality of sets of real numbers lead to definitions of cardinal characteristics, such as the least size of a set of reals of positive Lebesgue measure, or the least size of an unbounded set of functions from
\omega
to
\omega
. In studying such cardinal characteristics, one would usually work with perfect Polish spaces, such as the Baire space
{}^{\omega}\omega
, instead of directly working with the set of reals.

In recent years, there have been significant developments in generalising the theory of cardinal characteristics of the continuum to the context of the generalised Baire space
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, where
\kappa
is an uncountable cardinal. In many cases, the situation on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
closely resembles the situation on
{}^{\omega}\omega
, but this is not always the case. For instance, the meagre ideal
\mathcal{M}
, as defined on
{}^{\omega}\omega
, generalises without much trouble to a
\kappa
-meagre ideal on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, but the ideal
\mathcal{N}
of Lebesgue null sets does not; after all, there is no adequate notion of a Lebesgue measure on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
. Another important difference, is the rich structure of the club filter and of stationary sets on
\kappa
, which is nonexistent on
\omega
.

Due to the lack of a Lebesgue measure, and hence a null ideal, it is unclear what analogue in the generalised Baire space would fit for cardinal characteristics defined in terms of the null ideal. In the classical Baire space
{}^{\omega}\omega
there is a characterisation of the cardinal characteristics
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{N})
in terms of slaloms, that is, functions
\varphi:\omega\to\mathcal{P}(\omega)
such that
|\varphi(n)|=2^{n}
for each
n\in\omega
. Specifically,
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{N})
is equal to the least size of a set
W
of slaloms such that every
f\in{}^{\omega}\omega
is localised by a slalom in
W
, that is,
f(n)\in\varphi(n)
for all but finitely many
n
. This combinatorially defined cardinal characteristic is usually named the localisation number.

We will consider a parametrised localisation number, where we consider a set of slaloms that localises all
f\in{}^{\omega}\omega
, with the additional requirement that each slalom
\varphi
in our witness set
W
is bound by a function
h\in{}^{\omega}\omega
, in the sense that
|\varphi(n)|\leq h(n)
for all
n\in\omega
. The effect of this parameter
h
is rather limited on
{}^{\omega}\omega
, since the
h
-localisation cardinal is equal to
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{N})
for any parameter
h
that is increasing and cofinal, and thus its cardinality does not depend on the choice of
h
. On the generalised Baire space
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
for
\kappa
inaccessible, the situation is very different, as it is consistent that there exist many parametrised localisation numbers of different cardinalities, as shown by Brendle, Brooke-Taylor, Friedman & Montoya in
[
BBTFM18
]
and improved by the author in
[
vdV23
]
.

On
{}^{\omega}\omega
, we gain a significant amount of complexity when we do not consider cardinal characteristics defined on the entire space
{}^{\omega}\omega
, but on a bounded subspace
\prod_{n\in\omega}b(n)
for some increasing cofinal
b\in{}^{\omega}\omega
. This results in
(b,h)
-localisation numbers, defined with
(b,h)
-slaloms
\varphi
, where
|\varphi(n)|\leq h(n)
and
\varphi(n)\subseteq b(n)
for all
n\in\omega
, localising functions
f\in\prod_{n\in\omega}b(n)
. Such cardinals were first studied by Goldstern & Shelah
[
GS93
]
. Later, a connection between these cardinals, Yorioka ideals and the strong measure zero ideal was given by Osuga & Kamo
[
KO08
,
KO14
]
and it was shown recently by Cardona, Klausner & Mejía
[
CKM21
]
that there can be models with
2^{\aleph_{0}}
many
(b,h)
-localisation numbers with mutually distinct cardinality.

The goal of this article is to study such parametrised localisation numbers in the context of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
where
\kappa
is inaccessible. Alongside, we will also describe antilocalisation cardinals, whose (non-parametrised) cardinality gives a combinatorial definition of the cardinal characteristics
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M})
, and of parametrised versions of the dominating number and the eventual difference number (which also has non-parametrised versions that happen to be equal to
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M})
).

After introducing the notation and giving an overview of the situation on the (unbounded) Baire space in section
2
, we will use section
3
to investigate for which parameters
b
and
h
our cardinal numbers are nontrivial, in the sense that their values lie between
\kappa^{+}
and
2^{\kappa}
without being equal to either bound. Although we can describe sufficient conditions for the cardinals to be nontrivial, there are still several open problems concerning the necessity of these conditions.

In section
4
we will give an overview of how our cardinals relate to each other. We do this by giving Tukey connections between relational systems. We also introduce cardinal characteristics defined by taking the infimum or supremum of (anti)localisation cardinals over all possible parameters.

Finally, in section
5
we will prove consistency results regarding our cardinals. In particular we will construct a model in which there are
\kappa^{+}
many mutually distinct localisation cardinals (using the same type of forcing notion as in
[
vdV23
]
), and we describe a family of
\kappa
many parameters such that any finite set of parameters has associated antilocalisation cardinals that can be forced to be mutually distinct (using a generalisation of the forcing notion from
[
CM19
]
).

2. Notation & Definition of Concepts

Let us establish our notation. Usually we will use the Greek letters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\varepsilon,\eta,\xi
for ordinals, while
\kappa,\lambda,\mu,\nu
will be used for cardinals. The class of all ordinals is written as
\mathrm{Ord}
.

We say that a property
P
holds for
almost all

\alpha\in\kappa
if there is a
\beta\in\kappa
such that
P(\alpha)
holds for all
\alpha>\beta
, and we abbreviate this as
\forall^{\infty}\alpha P(\alpha)
. Dually, a property is said to hold for
cofinally many

\alpha\in\kappa
if for every
\beta\in\kappa
there is some
\alpha>\beta
for which
P(\alpha)
holds, abbreviated as
\exists^{\infty}\alpha P(\alpha)
.

Given two functions
f,f^{\prime}
with domain
\kappa
and
\vartriangleleft
a relation defined on
\mathrm{ran}(f)\times\mathrm{ran}(f^{\prime})
, we write

\bullet

f\mathrel{\vartriangleleft}f^{\prime}
as a shorthand for
\forall\alpha\in\kappa(f(\alpha)\mathrel{\vartriangleleft}f^{\prime}(\alpha))
,

\bullet

f\mathrel{\vartriangleleft}^{*}f^{\prime}
as a shorthand for
\forall^{\infty}\alpha(f(\alpha)\vartriangleleft f^{\prime}(\alpha))
,

\bullet

f\mathrel{\vartriangleleft}^{\infty}f^{\prime}
as a shorthand for
\exists^{\infty}\alpha(f(\alpha)\vartriangleleft f^{\prime}(\alpha))
.

The intended meaning of
\cancel{\vartriangleleft^{*}}
and
\cancel{\vartriangleleft^{\infty}}
are the negations of
\vartriangleleft^{*}
and
\vartriangleleft^{\infty}
respectively, as should be clear on sight, in contrast to the ambiguously notated
\not\hskip-0.85358pt\vartriangleleft^{*}
and
\not\hskip-0.85358pt\vartriangleleft^{\infty}
. For this reason we will henceforth use the former notation.

Unsurprisingly, we write cardinal exponentiation as
\lambda^{\mu}
and use the abbreviation
\lambda^{<\mu}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mu}\lambda^{|\alpha|}
. If instead we want to discuss the set of functions from
X
to
Y
, we write this as
{}^{X}Y
. If
\alpha
is an ordinal, then
{}^{<\alpha}Y
denotes the set
\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}{}^{\xi}Y
. We define

\displaystyle[Y]^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\in\mathcal{P}(Y)\mid|X|=\mu\right\}

\displaystyle[Y]^{<\mu}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\in\mathcal{P}(Y)\mid|X|<\mu\right\}.

Naturally,
{}^{\leq\alpha}Y
and
[Y]^{\leq\alpha}
have the obvious meaning that is implicit from the above.
Given sequences
s\in{}^{\alpha}X
and
t\in{}^{\beta}X
, we write
s^{\frown}t\in{}^{\alpha+\beta}X
for the concatenation of
s
and
t
. If
x\in X
, we write
\left\langle x\right\rangle
for the sequence of length
1
containing only
x
. If
s\in{}^{\alpha}X
is a sequence, we write
\mathrm{ot}(s)=\alpha
for the length or order-type of
s
. Similarly, if
A\subseteq\mathrm{Ord}
is a set of ordinals, we write
\mathrm{ot}(A)
for the order-type of
\left\langle A,\in\right\rangle
.

An increasing function
f:\kappa\to\mathrm{Ord}
is called
continuous
at
\gamma\in\kappa
if
f(\gamma)=\bigcup_{\alpha<\gamma}f(\alpha)
and
discontinuous
at
\gamma\in\kappa
if
f(\gamma)>\bigcup_{\alpha<\gamma}f(\alpha)
. If
A\subseteq\kappa
, then
f
is (dis)continuous on
A
if
f
is (dis)continuous at
\gamma
for every limit ordinal
\gamma\in A
.

If
f,g
are functions, we interpret arithmetical operators on the functions elementwise, such as
f+g:\alpha\mapsto f(\alpha)+g(\alpha)
and
2^{f}:\alpha\mapsto 2^{f(\alpha)}
and if
\xi
is an ordinal
f+\xi:\alpha\mapsto f(\alpha)+\xi
. We will often work with functions
b
where
b(\alpha)
is a cardinal for each
\alpha
. In such cases, we also establish that
\mathrm{cf}(b):\alpha\mapsto\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))
and
b^{+}:\alpha\mapsto(b(\alpha))^{+}
. Finally if
\alpha
is an ordinal, we write
\overline{\alpha}
for the constant function
\kappa\to\left\{\alpha\right\}
.

2.1. Bounded Generalised Baire Spaces

As the name suggests, the
generalised Baire space

{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is a generalisation of the classical Baire space
{}^{\omega}\omega
. Instead of functions between the natural numbers, we consider functions between uncountable cardinals. In our case, we are specifically interested in generalised Baire spaces where
\kappa
is a (strongly) inaccessible cardinal. As such, we will fix the convention for the remainder of this article that
\kappa
is
inaccessible
. We would like to emphasise that
we leave out the mention of
\kappa
being inaccessible from our lemmas and theorems
, even if this is a necessary assumption for the theorem to be provable.

We restrict our attention to inaccessible cardinals, because the Cichoń diagram on
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
behaves quite similar to its counterpart on
{}^{\omega}\omega
. We refer to
[
BBTFM18
]
for an overview of the Cichoń diagram on the generalised Baire space and to
[
Bre22
]
for the case where
\kappa
is not inaccessible.

The generalised Baire space
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is often studied as a topological space by endowing
\kappa
with the discrete topology and
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
with the
{<}\kappa
-box topology. This is the topology generated by basic open sets of the form
[s]=\left\{f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\mid s\subseteq f\right\}
, where
s\in{}^{\alpha}\kappa
for some
\alpha\in\kappa
. Note that each basic set
[s]
is in fact clopen.

The main focus of this article will be to study cardinal characteristics in the context of bounded subspaces of the generalised Baire space. Given a function
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, we consider the product space
\prod b=\prod_{\alpha\in\kappa}b(\alpha)
where each
b(\alpha)
has the discrete topology and
\prod b
once again has the
{<}\kappa
-box topology. We will write

\displaystyle\textstyle\prod_{<\kappa}b=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\kappa}\left(\prod_{\alpha\in\gamma}b(\alpha)\right),

then the topology on
\prod b
is generated by sets
[s]=\left\{f\in\prod b\mid s\subseteq f\right\}
with
s\in\prod_{<\kappa}b
. As such, we see that
\prod b
is a closed subspace of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
. We will refer to spaces of the form
\prod b
as
bounded generalised Baire spaces
. Generally we will only consider
increasing
functions
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
b(\alpha)
is an
infinite cardinal
for every
\alpha\in\kappa
, unless we state otherwise.

A set
X\subseteq\prod b
is
nowhere dense
if for every basic open
[s]\subseteq\prod b
there exists a basic open
[t]\subseteq[s]
such that
[t]\cap X=\varnothing
. It is easy to show that the closure of a nowhere dense set is nowhere dense. The complement of a closed nowhere dense set is an
open dense set
. We will write
\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}^{b}
for the family of nowhere dense subsets of
\prod b
. A set
X
is
\kappa
-meagre
if there exists a family
\left\{N_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\}
of nowhere dense sets such that
X=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}N_{\alpha}
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b}
be the set of
\kappa
-meagre subsets of
\prod b
. We will use the notation
\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}
for the family
\kappa
-meagre sets of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Given a
{\leq}\kappa
-complete ideal
\mathcal{I}
on the space
X
, we define the following four cardinal functions:

\displaystyle\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\left\{|\mathcal{C}|\ \middle|\ \mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\land\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{C}=X\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\left\{|N|\ \middle|\ N\subseteq X\land N\notin\mathcal{I}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\left\{|\mathcal{A}|\ \middle|\ \mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\land\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{C}\notin\mathcal{I}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle=\min\left\{|\mathcal{F}|\ \middle|\ \mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\land\forall I\in\mathcal{I}\exists J\in\mathcal{F}(I\subseteq J)\right\}

It is easy to show that families
\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b}
(and
\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
are
{\leq}\kappa
-complete proper ideals. Moreover, by the following lemma, the choice of
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
does not matter for the value of these cardinal functions evaluated over
\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b}
, and indeed, we could work with the cardinal functions evaluated over
\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}
without loss of generality.

Lemma 2.1

There exists
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b}
such that the subspace
\prod b\setminus M
of
\prod b
is homeomorphic to
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

First, we recursively define a function
\varphi:{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\to\prod_{<\kappa}b
. Let
\varphi(\varnothing)=\varnothing
. Given
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
such that
\varphi(t)
has been defined, let
A=\left\{s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\}\subseteq\prod_{<\kappa}b
be an antichain of size
\kappa
with
\varphi(t)\subseteq s_{\alpha}
, such that
A
is maximal with this property, and send
\varphi:t^{\frown}\left\langle\alpha\right\rangle\mapsto s_{\alpha}
. If
\gamma=\mathrm{dom}(t)
is limit and
\varphi(t\restriction\alpha)
is defined for each
\alpha<\gamma
, we have by construction that

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigcup_{\alpha\in\gamma}\varphi(t\restriction\alpha)\in\prod_{<\kappa}b.

Therefore we set
\varphi(t)=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\gamma}\varphi(t\restriction\alpha)
.

Note that
\varphi[{}^{\alpha}\kappa]
forms a maximal antichain in
\prod_{<\kappa}b
: if
\alpha
is the least ordinal such that
\varphi[{}^{\alpha}\kappa]
is not maximal, let
s\notin\varphi[{}^{\alpha}\kappa]
be such that
\varphi[{}^{\alpha}\kappa]\cup\left\{s\right\}
is an antichain, then for any
\xi<\alpha
there is
s_{\xi}\subseteq s
such that
s_{\xi}\in\varphi[{}^{\xi}\kappa]
, but then
\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}s_{\xi}=s^{\prime}\subseteq s
and
s^{\prime}\in\varphi[{}^{\alpha}\kappa]
.

We define
\Phi:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to\prod b
induced by
\varphi
as sending
f\mapsto\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}\varphi(f\restriction\alpha)
. Note that the range
\Phi[{}^{\kappa}\kappa]
is
\kappa
-comeagre in
\prod b
, because for each
\alpha\in\kappa
we can define

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}=\left\{f\in\textstyle\prod b\mid f\notin\bigcup_{t\in{}^{\alpha}\kappa}[\varphi(k)]\right\}.

Then
F_{\alpha}
is nowhere dense, since
\varphi[{}^{\alpha}\kappa]
is a maximal antichain, and
\Phi[{}^{\kappa}\kappa]=\prod b\setminus\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}F_{\alpha}
, hence
M=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}F_{\alpha}
is a
\kappa
-meagre set and we will see that
\Phi:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow}\prod b\setminus M
is a homeomorphism.

It is clear that
\Phi
is bijective. Note that
\Phi
is open, since for any
t\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
we have
\varphi(t)\in\prod_{<\kappa}b
, thus
\Phi[[t]]=[\varphi(t)]\setminus M
is open in
\prod b\setminus M
. To see that
\Phi
is continuous, let
s\in\prod_{<\kappa}b
with
\mathrm{dom}(s)=\alpha
. If
t\in{}^{\alpha}\kappa
, then it follows by construction that
\alpha\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(\varphi(t))
. Let

\displaystyle T=\left\{t\in{}^{\alpha}\kappa\mid s\subseteq\varphi(t)\right\}.

Note that
f\in\bigcup_{t\in T}[t]
if and only if
s\subseteq\varphi(f\restriction\alpha)
off
\Phi(f)\in[s]
if and only if
f\in\Phi^{-1}[[s]]
.
∎

Corollary 2.2

\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b})
,

\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b})
,

\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b})
,

\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa}^{b})
.

Apart from the
\kappa
-meagre ideal, we will also consider the ideal of
\kappa
-strong measure zero sets. Contrary to Lebesgue measure zero sets, strong measure zero sets can be generalised to the context of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
. We will work in
{}^{\kappa}2
, with basic open sets
[s]=\left\{f\in{}^{\kappa}2\mid s\subseteq f\right\}
for
s\in{}^{<\kappa}2
. A set
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}2
is called
\kappa
-strong measure zero
if for every
f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
there exists a sequence
\left\langle s_{\alpha}\in{}^{<\kappa}2\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
with
\mathrm{ot}(s_{\alpha})=f(\alpha)
such that
X\subseteq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}[s_{\alpha}]
. We write
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
for the family of
\kappa
-strong measure zero subsets of
{}^{\kappa}2
.

It is again easy to see that
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
is a
{\leq}\kappa
-complete proper ideal. We conclude this section by giving an equivalent definition of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
.

Lemma 2.3

The following are equivalent for a set
X\subseteq{}^{\kappa}2
.

(1)

X\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}

(2)

For every
f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
there exists a sequence
\overline{s}=\left\langle s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
with
s_{\alpha}\in{}^{f(\alpha)}2
for each
\alpha
such that
X\subseteq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}[s_{\alpha}]
.

(3)

For every
f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
there exists a sequence
\overline{s}=\left\langle s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
with
s_{\alpha}\in{}^{f(\alpha)}2
for each
\alpha
such that
X\subseteq\bigcap_{\alpha_{0}\in\kappa}\bigcup_{\beta\in[\alpha_{0},\kappa)}[s_{\beta}]
.

Note that (2) is simply the definition of elements of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
. That (3) implies (2) is obvious.

To see that (2) implies (3), let
\pi:\kappa\times\kappa\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow}\kappa
be a bijection and
f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
. We define
f_{\xi}\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
by
f_{\xi}(\alpha)=f(\pi(\xi,\alpha))
and use (2) to find a sequence
\overline{s}^{\xi}=\langle s_{\alpha}^{\xi}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\rangle
with
s_{\alpha}^{\xi}\in{}^{f_{\xi}(\alpha)}2
such that
X\subseteq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}[s^{\xi}_{\alpha}]
. Given
\xi,\alpha\in\kappa
, we define
s_{\pi(\xi,\alpha)}=s_{\alpha}^{\xi}
then
s_{\beta}\in f(\beta)
for all
\beta\in\kappa
. It follows that
X\subseteq\bigcap_{\alpha_{0}\in\kappa}\bigcup_{\beta\in[\alpha_{0},\kappa)}[s_{\beta}]
.
∎If
\overline{s}=\left\langle s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
is a sequence such that
X\subseteq\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}[s^{\xi}_{\alpha}]
, we say
X
is
covered
by
\overline{s}
. If also
X\subseteq\bigcap_{\alpha_{0}\in\kappa}\bigcup_{\beta\in[\alpha_{0},\kappa)}[s_{\beta}]
, then we say
X
is
cofinally covered
by
\overline{s}
.

2.2. Cardinal Characteristics & Relational Systems

In order to study the relations between our cardinal characteristics, we will make use of relational systems. All of the cardinal characteristics we are interested in, can be expressed as the norm of a relational system, thus we will use the language of relational systems to define our cardinal characteristics. We will only give a concise description of relational systems below, and refer to
[
Bla10
]
for a detailed description.

A relational system
\mathscr{R}=\left\langle X,Y,R\right\rangle
is a triple of sets
X
and
Y
and a relation
R\subseteq X\times Y
. We define the
norm
of
\mathscr{R}
as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{R}\right\rVert=\min\left\{|W|\mid W\subseteq Y\text{ and }\forall x\in X\exists y\in W(x\mathrel{R}y)\right\}.

We refer to a set
W\subseteq Y
such that
\forall x\in X\exists y\in W(x\mathrel{R}y)
as a
witness
for
\left\lVert\mathscr{R}\right\rVert\leq|W|
. We define a
dual
relation to
R
by

\displaystyle\cancel{R}^{-1}=\left\{(y,x)\in Y\times X\mid(x,y)\notin R\right\}.

Correspondingly we can define the dual relational system
\mathscr{R}^{\bot}=\left\langle Y,X,\cancel{R}^{-1}\right\rangle
.

Intuitively, we can see
Y
as a set of possible responses and
X
as the set of potential challenges. We want to find a set of responses
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
such that every challenge can be met with a response from
Y^{\prime}
, and the norm expresses the least number of responses necessary to do so. The dual relational system answers the question how many challenges we should gather such that no single response can meet them all.

Given two relational systems
\mathscr{R}=\left\langle X,Y,R\right\rangle
and
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}=\left\langle X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},R^{\prime}\right\rangle
, a
Tukey connection
from
\mathscr{R}
to
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
is a pair of functions
\rho_{-}:X\to X^{\prime}
and
\rho_{+}:Y^{\prime}\to Y
such that for any
x\in X
and
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
with
(\rho_{-}(x),y^{\prime})\in R^{\prime}
we also have
(x,\rho_{+}(y^{\prime}))\in R
. We let
\mathscr{R}\preceq\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
denote the claim that there exists a Tukey connection from
\mathscr{R}
to
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
, and we let
\mathscr{R}\equiv\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
abbreviate
\mathscr{R}\preceq\mathscr{R}^{\prime}\preceq\mathscr{R}
. Note that if
\left\langle\rho_{-},\rho_{+}\right\rangle
witnesses
\mathscr{R}\preceq\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
, then
\left\langle\rho_{+},\rho_{-}\right\rangle
witnesses
\mathscr{R}^{\prime\bot}\preceq\mathscr{R}^{\bot}
.

We will use Tukey connections to give an ordering between the norms of relational systems, through the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4

If
\mathscr{R}\preceq\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
, then
\left\lVert\mathscr{R}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\mathscr{R}^{\prime}\right\rVert
and
\left\lVert\mathscr{R}^{\prime\bot}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\mathscr{R}^{\bot}\right\rVert
.

We will on one occasion require a composition of several relational systems. In particular we need a general form of the categorical product that is defined in
[
Bla10
]
. We will state the definition and give two lemmas that are useful to compute the norm.

Let
\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}=\left\langle X_{\alpha},Y_{\alpha},R_{\alpha}\right\rangle
be relational systems for each
\alpha\in A
, where
A
is a set of ordinals (but we will omit mention of
A
from now on for the sake of brevity). We define the categorical product as the system
\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}=\left\langle\bigcup_{\alpha}(X_{\alpha}\times\left\{\alpha\right\}),\prod_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha},Z\right\rangle
, where
(x,\alpha)\mathrel{Z}\overline{y}
off
x\mathrel{R}_{\alpha}\overline{y}(\alpha)
. Dually we have the categorical coproduct
\bigoplus_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}=(\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}^{\bot})^{\bot}=\left\langle\prod_{\alpha}X_{\alpha},\bigcup_{\alpha}(Y_{\alpha}\times\left\{\alpha\right\}),N\right\rangle
, where
\overline{x}\mathrel{N}(y,\alpha)
iff
\overline{x}(\alpha)\mathrel{R}_{\alpha}y
.

Lemma 2.5

\left\lVert\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert=\sup_{\alpha}\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
.

(
\leq
) Let
Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}\subseteq Y_{\alpha}
be a witness for
|Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}|=\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
. Let
\lambda=\sup_{\alpha}\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
and let
\sigma_{\alpha}:\lambda\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}
be surjections. Define
\overline{y}_{\xi}:\alpha\mapsto\sigma_{\alpha}(\xi)
and let
Y=\left\{\overline{y}_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\lambda\right\}\subseteq\prod_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
. If
x\in X_{\alpha}
, let
y\in Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}
such that
x\mathrel{R}_{\alpha}y
and find
\xi\in\lambda
such that
y=\sigma_{\alpha}(\xi)
, then
(x,\alpha)\mathrel{Z}\overline{y}_{\xi}
. Hence
Y
witnesses that
\left\lVert\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert\leq\lambda
.

(
\geq
) Let
Y\subseteq\prod_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
be a witness for
|Y|=\left\lVert\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
, and let
Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\left\{\overline{y}(\alpha)\mid\overline{y}\in Y\right\}\subseteq Y_{\alpha}
. Then for every
x\in X_{\alpha}
there exists
\overline{y}\in Y
such that
(x,\alpha)\mathrel{Z}\overline{y}
, hence
\overline{y}(\alpha)\in Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}
has the property that
x\mathrel{R}_{\alpha}\overline{y}(\alpha)
, thus
\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert\leq|Y^{\prime}|\leq|Y|=\left\lVert\bigotimes_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
.
∎

Lemma 2.6

\left\lVert\bigoplus_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert=\inf_{\alpha}\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
.

(
\leq
) Let
Y\subseteq Y_{\alpha}
be a witness for
|Y|=\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
. Let
Y^{\prime}=\left\{(y,\alpha)\mid y\in Y\right\}
. If
\overline{x}\in\prod_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
, then there exists
y\in Y
such that
\overline{x}(\alpha)\mathrel{R}_{\alpha}y
and thus
\overline{x}\mathrel{N}(y,\alpha)
. Therefore
\left\lVert\bigoplus_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert\leq|Y^{\prime}|=|Y|=\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
.

(
\geq
) Let
Y\subseteq\bigcup_{\alpha}(Y_{\alpha}\times\left\{\alpha\right\})
with
|Y|<\inf_{\alpha}\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
. Let
Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\left\{y\in Y_{\alpha}\mid(y,\alpha)\in Y\right\}
. Since
|Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}|<\left\lVert\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
there is
x_{\alpha}\in X_{\alpha}
such that
x_{\alpha}\mathrel{\cancel{R}}_{\alpha}y
for all
y\in Y^{\prime}_{\alpha}
. Let
\overline{x}:\alpha\mapsto x_{\alpha}
, then
\overline{x}\mathrel{\cancel{N}}(y,\alpha)
for every
(y,\alpha)\in Y
. Thus
|Y|<\left\lVert\bigoplus_{\alpha}\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\right\rVert
.
∎

Apart from the previously mentioned cardinal functions on ideals, we will define four other families of cardinals. In order to do so, we need to discuss
slaloms
, a concept first formulated by Bartoszyński to give a combinatorial characterisation of the additivity and cofinality of the Lebesgue null ideal in the classical Baire space (see
[
Bar87
,
BJ95
]
or the next subsection).

Definition 2.7

Let
h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be an increasing function such that
h(\alpha)
is a nonzero cardinal for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. We will generally assume
h\leq b
. Let
\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
be the set of functions
\varphi
with
\mathrm{dom}(\varphi)=\kappa
such that
\varphi(\alpha)\subseteq b(\alpha)
and
\left|\varphi(\alpha)\right|<h(\alpha)
for each
\alpha\in\kappa
. If
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, then we call
\varphi
a
(b,h)
-slalom
.
1
1

1

Note that this definition differs from the usual definition: we define
\varphi
such that
|\varphi(\alpha)|<h(\alpha)\leq b(\alpha)
, instead of the traditional
|\varphi(\alpha)|\leq h(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
. Our definition is more versatile. For example, if
h(\alpha)
is a limit cardinal for each
\alpha
, the resulting set of all slaloms
\varphi
with
|\varphi(\alpha)|<h(\alpha)
cannot be expressed using the traditional definition. On the other hand, the set of all traditionally defined
(b,h)
-slaloms is the set of
(b,h^{+})
-slaloms under our definition.

Given
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
and
f\in\prod b
, then we say
\varphi

localises

f
iff
f\in^{*}\varphi
and
\varphi

antilocalises

f
iff
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi
.

Similar to how we fixed the notation
\prod_{<\kappa}b
for initial segments of functions in
\prod b
, we will fix the notation
\mathrm{Loc}_{<\kappa}^{b,h}
for initial segments of
(b,h)
-slaloms, that is,
s\in\mathrm{Loc}_{<\kappa}^{b,h}
if and only if
\mathrm{dom}(s)\in\kappa
and for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(s)
we have
s(\alpha)\in[b(\alpha)]^{<h(\alpha)}
.

We now define the following four relational systems and associated cardinals:

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{b}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\textstyle\prod b,\prod b,\leq^{*}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{D}_{b}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathscr{D}_{b}^{\bot}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\textstyle\prod b,\prod b,\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}^{\bot}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{b,h}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\textstyle\prod b,\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h},\in^{*}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathscr{L}_{b,h}^{\bot}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h},\textstyle\prod b,\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}^{\bot}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

We call
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
the
dominating
number,
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
the
unbounding
number,
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
the
eventual difference
number,
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
the
cofinal equality
number
2
2

2

In the classical setting, the relation of being cofinally equal is often referred to as being infinitely equal, but in our generalised setting we require more than just an infinite amount of equality.
. Both
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
are sometimes called
localisation
cardinals, but we believe it will be useful to distinguish between these classes of cardinals with separate terms. We therefore opt to call
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
the
localisation
cardinal, and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
the
avoidance
cardinal, for the reason that a witness to
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
is a set of functions
F\subseteq\prod b
that cannot be localised by a single
(b,h)
-slalom; localisation by any specific slalom is always avoided by at least one member of
F
. Finally this leaves the cardinals
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
, which we will call the
anti-avoidance cardinal
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
, which we will call the
antilocalisation
cardinal.

It should be mentioned that (anti)localisation cardinals not only suffer from ambiguous names, they also suffer from nonuniformity of notation. In previous literature, the notations
\mathfrak{c}_{b,h}^{\forall}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{b,h}^{\forall}
have also frequently been used for
\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
respectively, while
\mathfrak{c}_{b,h}^{\exists}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{b,h}^{\exists}
have been used for
\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
respectively. For example, this notation was used in
[
Kel08
,
KO14
,
KM21
,
CKM21
]
. Here
\mathfrak{c}
stands for a
c
over of
\prod b
with slaloms by the relations
\in^{*}
or
\in^{\infty}
, and
\mathfrak{v}
stands for a
v
oidance by or e
v
asion by a single slalom. We believe that
avoidance
forms the better antonym to
localisation
, as it prevents confusion with other cardinal characteristics known as evasion cardinals (such as described in the eponymous section of
[
Bla10
]
). Our choice to use the notation
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
over
\mathfrak{c}_{b,h}^{\forall}
has the main benefit that the relevant relational system can be deduced from our notation. Finally, we opted to use the relation
\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}}
instead of
\in^{\infty}
in our relational system for antilocalisation, since we will later see that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
bears more similarities to
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
than to
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.

2.3. Classical and Higher Cichoń Diagram

In the next section we will investigate the provable relations between the cardinals we have defined and what influence the parameters
b
and
h
have on their cardinality. Before doing so, we would like to discuss what is known about the case where we let
b=\overline{\kappa}
be the constant function, in other words, what is known about these cardinals in the (unbounded) generalised Baire space, and how this compares to the same cardinals as defined classically on
{}^{\omega}\omega
. When discussing the cardinals in the context of the entire space
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, we simply leave out the parameter
b
from the notation, e.g.
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
instead of
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\kappa}}(\leq^{*})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
instead of
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\kappa},h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
. When discussing the cardinal in context of the classical Baire space, we replace
\kappa
by
\omega
, e.g.
\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
.

In the context of the classical Baire space
{}^{\omega}\omega
, each of the cardinals mentioned so far is an element of the Cichoń diagram. Let
\mathcal{N}
denote the ideal of sets of reals with negligible Lebesgue measure, and let
\mathcal{M}
denote the (
\omega
-)meagre ideal on
{}^{\omega}\omega
. The Cichoń diagram gives an overview of the relations between our cardinals, where
\mathfrak{x}\leq\mathfrak{y}
is provable in
\mathsf{ZFC}
if and only if there exists a path from
\mathfrak{x}
to
\mathfrak{y}
following the arrows.

\aleph_{1}
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{N})
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M})
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{N})
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M})
\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}(\leq^{*})
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M})
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{N})
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M})
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{N})
2^{\aleph_{0}}

Proofs for these relations and for the following facts can also be found in
[
BJ95
]
or
[
Bla10
]
.

Fact 2.8

( ​​
[
Tru77
,
Mil81
]
)

\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}(\leq^{*}),\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M})\right\}
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}(\leq^{*}),\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M})\right\}

Fact 2.9

( ​​
[
Bar87
]
)

\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M})=\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M})=\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{N})=\mathfrak{d}^{h}_{\omega}(\in^{*})
and
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M})=\mathfrak{b}^{h}_{\omega}(\in^{*})
for any increasing cofinal
h\in{}^{\omega}\omega
.

In the context of the generalised Baire space
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
(where we assume, as mentioned, that
\kappa
is inaccessible), we do not have Lebesgue measure, thus the four cardinal invariants related to
\mathcal{N}
cannot be generalised. The other cardinals can be generalised, as we have defined in the previous section, and the Cichoń diagram looks identical to the classical case if we assume that
\kappa
is strongly inaccessible
3
3

3

This assumption is necessary for all of the cardinals of (the middle part of) the Cichoń diagram to be nontrivial, but there are some interesting questions for
\kappa
being accessible, see
[
Bre22
]
.
and
h
is a cofinal increasing function.

\kappa^{+}
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
2^{\kappa}

Proofs for these relations can be found in
[
BBTFM18
]
.

Fact 2.10

( ​​
[
BBTFM18
]
)

\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})\right\}
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})\right\}

Fact 2.11

( ​​
[
BHZ05
]
,
[
Lan92
]
)

\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
.

Contrary to the classical case, the parameter
h
of
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
matters in the generalised case. If
\mathsf{id}:\alpha\mapsto|\alpha|
and
\mathsf{pow}:\alpha\mapsto 2^{|\alpha|}
, then the corresponding cardinals are consistently different:

Fact 2.12

( ​​
[
BBTFM18
]
)

It is consistent that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{pow}}(\in^{*})<\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{id}}(\in^{*})
.

This consistency result can be improved considerably.

Fact 2.13

( ​​
[
vdV23
]
)

(Consistently) there exists a family
H\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
with
|H|=\kappa^{+}
such that for any enumeration
\left\langle h_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa^{+}\right\rangle
of
H
there exists a forcing extension in which
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h_{\xi}}(\in^{*})<\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h_{\xi^{\prime}}}(\in^{*})
for all
\xi<\xi^{\prime}
.

A dual result to the above would be the consistency of
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{id}}(\in^{*})<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{\mathsf{pow}}(\in^{*})
, but the forcing techniques used to give the above-mentioned consistency results cannot be dualised, and other forcing methods are hard to use due to a lack of preservation theorems in the context of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Meanwhile, the parameter
h
does not influence the cardinality of
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
or
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
, since we have the following result, which we will prove with
4.4
.

Fact 2.14

\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
for any choice of
h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

We will see that the parameter
b
does make a difference, and that there can be consistently different cardinalities of the form
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
for different values of
b,h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

3. Trivial Choices of Parameters

It is perhaps not very surprising that some choices of parameters
b
and
h
will result in the cardinal characteristics having trivial values. What we mean precisely with a cardinal characteristic
\mathfrak{x}
having a ‘trivial’ value, is that
\mathfrak{x}
is undefined, or that it is provable in
\text{``}\,\mathsf{ZFC}+\kappa\text{ is inaccessible}\,\text{''}
that
\mathfrak{x}\leq\kappa^{+}
or
\mathfrak{x}=2^{\kappa}
.

It is perhaps not evident that the determination of
b
and
h
such that our cardinals are nontrivial, is itself not a trivial task. Indeed, this section contains several nontrivial open questions regarding the triviality of cardinal characteristics.

We will establish a general pattern that it is possible to give a complete characterisation of the cases in which the cardinals
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
,
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
,
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
are trivial. For each of these families of cardinals we are able to formulate a trichotomy of the case where the cardinal is
{<}\kappa
, the case where it is exactly
\kappa
and the case where the cardinal is
{>}\kappa
. In the last case we can show that the cardinal is nontrivial and we give an independence proof.

For the cardinals
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
,
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
,
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
, the natural conjecture is that these are trivial exactly when their
\mathfrak{b}
-duals are trivial, but this turns out to be hard to prove in each case. We will give partial results and some related problems. On the other hand, we can show that these
\mathfrak{d}
-side cardinals are nontrivial whenever the
\mathfrak{b}
-side cardinals are nontrivial.

3.1. Domination & Unboundedness

Let us start with
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
. It is clear that value of these cardinals only depends on the cofinality of
b(\alpha)
by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1

\mathscr{D}_{b}\equiv\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{cf}(b)}
.

For each
\alpha\in\kappa
let
\langle\beta^{\alpha}_{\xi}\mid\xi<\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))\rangle
be a strictly increasing sequence of ordinals that is cofinal in
b(\alpha)
and for any
\eta\in b(\alpha)
let
\xi^{\alpha}_{\eta}=\min\{\xi\in\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))\mid\eta\leq\beta^{\alpha}_{\xi}\}

For
\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{cf}(b)}
let
\rho_{-}(f):\alpha\mapsto\xi^{\alpha}_{f(\alpha)}
and
\rho_{+}(g^{\prime}):\alpha\mapsto\beta^{\alpha}_{g^{\prime}(\alpha)}
.

For
\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{cf}(b)}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
let
\rho_{-}(f^{\prime}):\alpha\mapsto\beta^{\alpha}_{f^{\prime}(\alpha)}
and
\rho_{+}(g):\alpha\mapsto\xi^{\alpha}_{g(\alpha)}
.
∎

It also becomes apparent quite immediately why we require
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
to be such that
b(\alpha)
is an infinite cardinal for (almost) all
\alpha\in\kappa
:

Lemma 3.2

If
b(\alpha)
is a successor ordinal for almost all
\alpha
, then
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})=1
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
is undefined.

Let
b(\alpha)=f(\alpha)+1
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
, then clearly
f
dominates all functions in
\prod b
, hence
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})=1
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
is undefined.
∎

The following lemma gives a complete characterisation of the functions
b
for which
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
is trivial. Note that the cases (i), (ii) and (iii) form a trichotomy.

Lemma 3.3

For each regular cardinal
\lambda<\kappa
let
D_{\lambda}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))=\lambda\right\}
.

(i)

If there exists a least regular cardinal
\lambda<\kappa
such that
D_{\lambda}
is cofinal in
\kappa
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})=\lambda
.

(ii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all regular
\lambda<\kappa
and there exists a stationary set
S
such that for each
\xi\in S
there exists
\alpha_{\xi}\geq\xi
with
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha_{\xi}))\leq\xi
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})=\kappa
.

(iii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all regular
\lambda<\kappa
and there exists a club set
C
such that for each
\xi\in C
we have
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))>\xi
for all
\alpha\geq\xi
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})\geq\kappa^{+}
.

(i) For each
\gamma\in D_{\lambda}
let
\left\langle\delta_{\gamma}^{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\lambda\right\rangle
be an increasing cofinal sequence in
\gamma
. Let
f_{\alpha}\in\prod b
be any function such that
f_{\alpha}(\gamma)=\delta_{\gamma}^{\alpha}
for each
\gamma\in D_{\lambda}
, then we claim that
B=\left\{f_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\lambda\right\}
is unbounded. Let
g\in\prod b
. By the pigeonhole principle there exists
\alpha\in\lambda
such that
g(\gamma)<\delta_{\gamma}^{\alpha}
for cofinally many
\gamma\in D_{\lambda}
, hence we see that
f_{\alpha}\mathrel{\cancel{\leq^{*}}}g
.

On the other hand, if
|B|<\lambda
, then let
\alpha_{0}
be large enough such that
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))\geq\lambda
for all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, then
|\left\{f(\alpha)\mid f\in B\right\}|<\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))
for all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, thus we can pick
g\in\prod b
such that
g(\alpha)=\sup\left\{f(\alpha)\mid f\in B\right\}<b(\alpha)
for each
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
to see that
f\leq^{*}g
for all
f\in B
.

(ii) Since each
D_{\lambda}
is bounded, we may assume that
\alpha_{\xi}\neq\alpha_{\xi^{\prime}}
for each
\xi\in S
. For each
\xi\in S
, let
\{\beta_{\xi}^{\eta}\mid\eta\in\xi\}
be a cofinal subset of
b(\alpha_{\xi})
(not necessarily increasing). Given
\alpha\in\kappa
we define
f_{\alpha}
such that
f_{\alpha}(\alpha_{\xi})=\beta_{\xi}^{\alpha}
for all
\xi\in S
with
\alpha<\xi
and arbitrary otherwise. We claim that
B=\left\{f_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\}
is unbounded.

Let
g\in\prod b
, then for each
\xi\in S
there is a minimal
\eta_{\xi}<\xi
such that
g(\alpha_{\xi})<\beta_{\xi}^{\eta_{\xi}}=f_{\eta_{\xi}}(\alpha_{\xi})
, hence
g^{\prime}:\xi\mapsto\eta_{\xi}
is a regressive function on
S
. Therefore there is stationary
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
such that
g^{\prime}
is constant on
S^{\prime}
, say with value
\eta
, then we see that
f_{\eta}\mathrel{\cancel{\leq^{*}}}g
.

On the other hand, suppose that
B\subseteq\prod B
with
\mu=|B|<\kappa
and let
\lambda>\mu
be regular, then
D_{\lambda}
is bounded, thus there exists
\alpha_{0}
such that
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))>\mu
for all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
. Let
g\in\prod b
be such that
g(\alpha)=\sup\left\{f(\alpha)\mid f\in B\right\}
for each
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
to see that
f\leq^{*}g
for all
f\in B
.

(iii) Let
B\subseteq\prod b
with
|B|=\kappa
and enumerate
B
as
\left\{f_{\eta}\mid\eta\in\kappa\right\}
. Let
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
be an increasing enumeration of
C
. We define
g(\alpha)=\bigcup_{\eta\in\xi}f_{\eta}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in[\alpha_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi+1})
and
\xi\in\kappa
, then
g(\alpha)
is the supremum of a sequence of length
\xi\leq\alpha_{\xi}<\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))
, hence
g\in\prod b
. Clearly
f_{\xi}\leq^{*}g
for each
\xi\in\kappa
, so
B
is bounded.
∎

Hence, we see that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
is trivial in cases (i) and (ii). We will later prove that
\kappa^{+}<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
is consistent in case (iii), but let us take a look at the dual
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
first.

If
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
behaves dually to
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
, then we expect
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})<2^{\kappa}
to be inconsistent in cases (i) and (ii). Remember that we assume that
b
is increasing, hence by
3.1
, we can reduce case (i) to the situation where
b
is a constant function with a regular cardinal as value. In other words, we have to study the dominating number as defined in the space
{}^{\kappa}\lambda
where
\lambda<\kappa
is a regular cardinal.

Note that even if we drop the assumption that
b
is increasing, the following Tukey connection shows that the behaviour on the space
{}^{\kappa}\lambda
is essentially the relevant part of the (in)consistency of
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})<2^{\kappa}
.

Lemma 3.4

If
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is a strictly increasing sequence and
b^{\prime}:\xi\mapsto b(\alpha_{\xi})
, then
\mathscr{D}_{b^{\prime}}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
.

Easy.
∎

Dominating numbers in the space
{}^{\kappa}\lambda
have been studied by many in the past. Brendle showed in the last section of
[
Bre22
]
(using different notation where the roles of
\kappa
and
\lambda
are reversed) that if
\lambda<\kappa
and
\lambda
is regular uncountable, then
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\lambda}}(\leq^{*})<2^{\kappa}
is actually consistent. An example for a model where this holds, is the model resulting from adding
\kappa^{++}
many
\mu
-Cohen reals over
\mathsf{GCH}
, where
\mu<\lambda
. Because this also destroys the inaccessibility of
\kappa
, we cannot use this in our context.

The question whether
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\lambda}}(\leq^{*})<2^{\kappa}
is consistent with
\kappa\geq 2^{<\lambda}
is mentioned as Question 16 in
[
Bre22
]
. Moreover, the special case where
\kappa=\omega_{1}
and
\lambda=\omega
is a famous open problem of Jech and Prikry
[
JP79
]
that is still unsolved almost half a century later. We will give a partial answer and prove that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\lambda}}(\leq^{*})=2^{\kappa}
when
\kappa
is inaccessible.

Theorem 3.5

If
\lambda<\kappa
is regular and
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))=\lambda
for cofinally many
\alpha\in\kappa
, then
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})=2^{\kappa}
.

We will delay the essential part of the proof of this theorem to the next subsection, since it will be a corollary of
3.18
, which states that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=2^{\kappa}
if
h(\alpha)=\lambda
for cofinally many
\alpha\in\kappa
. This is related to
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
through the following lemma.

Lemma 3.6

If
b=^{*}h
, then
\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
.

We define
\rho_{-}:\prod b\to\prod b
and
\rho_{+}:\prod b\to\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
as a Tukey connection for
\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
.

We will have
\rho_{+}
sending
g\in\prod b
to
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
where
\varphi(\alpha)=g(\alpha)
whenever
h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
and arbitrary otherwise. Since
h=^{*}b
and
g(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
, this is well-defined. Let
\rho_{-}:f\mapsto f+1
.

If
f,g\in\prod b
, let
\varphi=\rho_{+}(g)
. Then
f+1\leq^{*}g
implies
f<^{*}g
, hence
f\in^{*}\varphi
.
∎

Let
\lambda<\kappa
be regular and
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))=\lambda
for cofinally many
\alpha\in\kappa
. By
3.1
and
3.4
we may assume that
b(\alpha)=\lambda
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. Let
h=b
, then the conditions of
3.18
and
3.6
are satisfied, thus
2^{\kappa}=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})\leq 2^{\kappa}
.
∎

We currently do not know whether
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
also is trivial in case (ii) of
3.3
.

Question 3.7

Let
D_{\lambda}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))=\lambda\right\}
be bounded for all regular
\lambda<\kappa
and
S
be stationary such that for each
\xi\in S
there exists
\alpha_{\xi}\geq\xi
with
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha_{\xi}))\leq\xi
. Is
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})<2^{\kappa}
consistent?

To conclude this subsection, we will give an independence result to show that both
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
are nontrivial in case (iii). We prove this with a generalisation of Hechler forcing.

Definition 3.8

Let
C\subseteq\kappa
. We define
(b,C)
-Hechler forcing

\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
as the forcing notion with conditions
p=(s,f)
where
s\in\prod_{<\kappa}b
is such that
\mathrm{dom}(s)\in C
and
f:\kappa\setminus\mathrm{dom}(s)\to\kappa
is such that
f(\alpha)\in b(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(f)
, where the ordering is defined as
(t,g)\leq(s,f)
iff
s\subseteq t
and
f(\alpha)\leq g(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(g)
and
f(\alpha)\leq t(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(t)\setminus\mathrm{dom}(s)
.

It is clear from the definition, under the assumptions of case (iii), that
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
adds a generic element of
\prod b
that dominates all functions in
\prod b
from the ground model. We will show that
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
preserves cardinals and cofinalities. Since
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
is not
{<}\kappa
-closed in general, we need a slightly weaker variant of closure.

Definition 3.9

A forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
is
strategically
{<}\kappa
-closed
if White has a winning strategy for
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{P},p)
for all
p\in\mathbb{P}
. Here
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{P},p)
is the game of length
\kappa
as follows: at stage
\alpha\in\kappa
, White chooses a condition
p_{\alpha}
such that
p_{\alpha}\leq p^{\prime}_{\xi}
for all
\xi<\alpha
(and
p_{0}\leq p
) and Black subsequently chooses a condition
p^{\prime}_{\alpha}\leq p_{\alpha}
. White wins
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{P},p)
if White can make a move at every stage
\alpha\in\kappa
of the game.

Lemma 3.10

Let
C
be a club set such that for each
\xi\in C
we have
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))>\xi
for all
\alpha\geq\xi
, then
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
is strategically
{<}\kappa
-closed and
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c.

For strategic
{<}\kappa
-closure, at stage
\alpha
of the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C),p)
, let
\langle p_{\xi}\mid\xi<\alpha\rangle
and
\langle p^{\prime}_{\xi}\mid\xi<\alpha\rangle
be the sequences of previous moves by White and Black respectively. The winning strategy for White will be to choose
(s_{\alpha},f_{\alpha})
such that
\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})\geq\alpha
in successor stages. Under this strategy, if
\alpha
is a limit ordinal, then we have
\xi\leq\mathrm{dom}(s_{\xi})\in C
for each
\xi\in\alpha
. It follows that
\alpha\leq\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})
and hence
\mathrm{cf}(b(\beta))>\alpha
for all
\beta\geq\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})
by the properties of
C
. Therefore
\left\langle f_{\xi}(\beta)\mid\xi\in\alpha\right\rangle
is not cofinal in
b(\beta)
, thus we can define
f_{\alpha}:\beta\mapsto\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}f_{\xi}(\beta)
for each
\beta\in\kappa\setminus\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})
.

For
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c., note that for any
(s,f),(s,g)\in\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
we can choose
h(\alpha)=\max\left\{f(\alpha),g(\alpha)\right\}
for all
\alpha\in\kappa\setminus\mathrm{dom}(s)
to see that have
(s,h)\leq(s,f)
and
(s,h)\leq(s,g)
. Thus, if
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
is an antichain and
(s,f),(t,g)\in\mathcal{A}
are distinct, then we must have
s\neq t
, hence
|\mathcal{A}|\leq\kappa
.
∎

Corollary 3.11

\mathbb{D}^{b}_{\kappa}(C)
preserves cardinals and cofinality and does not add any elements to
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Fact 3.12

Iterations of arbitrary length with
{<}\kappa
-support preserve strategic
{<}\kappa
-closure and
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c., and hence preserve cardinals, cofinality and do not add any elements to
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

This implies especially that iteration will not destroy the inaccessibility of
\kappa
.

Theorem 3.13

Let
C
be a club set such that for each
\xi\in C
we have
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))>\xi
for all
\alpha\geq\xi
, then it is consistent that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})>\kappa^{+}
and that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})<2^{\kappa}
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa^{++}+\kappa^{+}\rangle
be a
{<}\kappa
-support iteration with
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}\Vdash\text{``}\,\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}=\dot{\mathbb{D}}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)\,\text{''}
for each
\alpha
and let
\mathbf{V}\vDash\text{``}\,\mathsf{GCH}\,\text{''}
. If
B\subseteq(\prod b)^{\mathbf{V}^{\mathbb{P}_{\kappa^{++}}}}
is such that
|B|\leq\kappa^{+}
, then
B\in\mathbf{V}^{\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}}
for some
\alpha<\kappa^{++}
, since the
\kappa^{++}
-th stage of the iteration does not add any
\kappa
-reals. The generic
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
-real that is added in the
\alpha+1
-th stage then dominates all elements of
B
, hence
B
is not unbounded. This shows that
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbb{P}_{\kappa^{++}}}\vDash\text{``}\,\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})>\kappa^{+}\,\text{''}
.

Next, let
D
consist of the
\kappa
-reals that are added in the final
\kappa^{+}
stages of the iteration, then
D
clearly forms a dominating family of size
\kappa^{+}
in
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbb{P}}
, and it is easy to see that
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbb{P}}\vDash\text{``}\,2^{\kappa}=\kappa^{++}\,\text{''}
, showing that
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbb{P}}\vDash\text{``}\,\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})<2^{\kappa}\,\text{''}
.
∎

3.2. Localisation & Avoidance

With localisation and avoidance cardinals, we have not only the parameter
b
, but also the parameter
h
giving the width of our slaloms. As with the dominating and unbounding numbers, there are certain choices of these parameters for which we have trivial cardinal characteristics.

We will motivate our assumption that
h\leq b
with the following two lemmas:

Lemma 3.14

b<^{*}h
if and only if
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=1
.

If
b<^{*}h
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
is undefined.

If
b<^{*}h
, let
B=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid b(\alpha)<h(\alpha)\right\}
, and choose some
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
such that
\varphi(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in B
. Since almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
are in
B
, we see that
f\in\prod b
implies
f\in^{*}\varphi
. Hence
\varphi
localises the entirety of
\prod b
, making
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=1
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
undefined.

Reversely, if there is a strictly increasing sequence
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
with
h(\alpha_{\xi})\leq b(\alpha_{\xi})
for all
\xi
, and
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, then there is
\gamma_{\xi}\in b(\alpha_{\xi})\setminus\varphi(\alpha_{\xi})
for each
\xi
since
|\varphi(\alpha_{\xi})|<h(\alpha_{\xi})\leq b(\alpha_{\xi})
. Therefore, if
f\in\prod b
is such that
f(\alpha_{\xi})=\gamma_{\xi}
for all
\xi\in\kappa
, then
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{*}}}\varphi
.
∎

We will state two essential properties relating two relational systems of the form
\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
with different parameters to each other. The first deals with cofinal subsets of indices (cf.
3.4
) and the second shows monotonicity with regards to
\leq^{*}
. The proofs are elementary.

Lemma 3.15

If
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is a strictly increasing sequence and
b^{\prime}:\xi\mapsto b(\alpha_{\xi})
and
h^{\prime}:\xi\mapsto h(\alpha_{\xi})
, then
\mathscr{L}_{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\preceq\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

Lemma 3.16

Let
h\leq^{*}h^{\prime}
and
b\geq^{*}b^{\prime}
, then there exist Tukey connections
{\mathscr{L}_{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}}\preceq{\mathscr{L}_{b,h}}
.

Mirroring the situation of
3.3
, we will give a complete characterisation of the cases in which
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
is trivial.

Lemma 3.17

For each
\lambda<\kappa
define
D_{\lambda}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid h(\alpha)=\lambda\right\}
.

(i)

If there exists a least cardinal
\lambda<\kappa
such that
D_{\lambda}
is cofinal in
\kappa
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=\lambda
.

(ii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all
\lambda<\kappa
and
h
is continuous on a stationary set, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=\kappa
,

(iii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all
\lambda<\kappa
and
h
is discontinuous on a club set, then
\kappa^{+}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.

(i) Assume that
\lambda
is minimal such that
D_{\lambda}
is cofinal. For each
\eta<\lambda
, define
f_{\eta}\in\prod b
elementwise by
f_{\eta}:\alpha\mapsto\eta
for all
\alpha\in D_{\lambda}
and
f_{\eta}:\alpha\mapsto 0
otherwise. If
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
and
\alpha\in D_{\lambda}
, then
|\varphi(\alpha)|<h(\alpha)=\lambda
, so there exists
\eta<\lambda
for which
\eta\notin\varphi(\alpha)
, hence
f_{\eta}\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{*}}}\varphi
. Therefore
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f_{\eta}\mid\eta<\lambda\right\}
witnesses that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})\leq|\mathcal{F}|=\lambda
.

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\prod b
and
|\mathcal{F}|<\lambda
, then by minimality of
\lambda
we see that
|\mathcal{F}|<h(\alpha)
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
, thus we can choose
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
such that
\varphi:\alpha\mapsto\left\{f(\alpha)\mid f\in\mathcal{F}\right\}
whenever
|\mathcal{F}|<h(\alpha)
, then we see that
f\in^{*}\varphi
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
, proving that
|\mathcal{F}|<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.

(ii) Since each
D_{\lambda}
is bounded, it follows that
\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid h(\alpha)\leq\lambda\right\}
is bounded for every
\lambda<\kappa
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\prod b
with
|\mathcal{F}|<\kappa
, and let
\beta\in\kappa
be such that
h(\alpha)\leq|\mathcal{F}|
implies
\alpha<\beta
. If
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
and
\xi\geq\beta
, then
|\mathcal{F}|<h(\xi)
, hence we can define
\varphi(\xi)=\left\{f(\xi)\mid f\in\mathcal{F}\right\}
for all
\xi\geq\beta
. It is clear that
f\in^{*}\varphi
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
, thus
|\mathcal{F}|<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
. Since
\mathcal{F}
was arbitrary such that
|\mathcal{F}|<\kappa
, we see that
\kappa\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.

To see
\kappa=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
, consider
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f_{\eta}\mid\eta\in\kappa\right\}
where
f_{\eta}:\alpha\mapsto\eta
when
\eta\in b(\alpha)
, and
f_{\eta}:\alpha\mapsto 0
otherwise. Since we assume
h\leq^{*}b
and
h
is cofinal, for every
\eta\in\kappa
there is
\alpha_{0}
such that
b(\alpha)>\eta
for all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, thus
f_{\eta}(\alpha)=\eta
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
. Since
h
is continuous on a stationary set
S_{0}
, the set of fixed-points
S_{1}=\left\{\alpha\in S_{0}\mid h(\alpha)=\alpha\right\}
is stationary, implying that
\alpha\mapsto|\varphi(\alpha)|
is regressive on the stationary
S_{1}
. Consequently Fodor’s lemma tells us that there exists a stationary set
S_{2}\subseteq S_{1}
and
\lambda\in\kappa
such that
|\varphi(\alpha)|<\lambda
for all
\alpha\in S_{2}
. If
f_{\eta}\in^{*}\varphi
for all
\eta\in\lambda
, then
N_{\eta}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid\eta\notin\varphi(\alpha)\right\}
is nonstationary for each
\eta\in\lambda
, so
\bigcup_{\eta\in\lambda}N_{\eta}
is nonstationary, and thus
S_{2}\setminus\bigcup_{\eta\in\lambda}N_{\eta}
is stationary. However, for any
\alpha\in S_{2}\setminus\bigcup_{\eta\in\lambda}N_{\eta}
we have
\lambda\subseteq\varphi(\alpha)
, which contradicts that
|\varphi(\alpha)|<\lambda
for
\alpha\in S_{2}
. Therefore
\mathcal{F}
forms a witness for
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})\leq\kappa
.

(iii) Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\prod b
with
|\mathcal{F}|=\kappa
and enumerate
\mathcal{F}
as
\left\langle f_{\eta}\mid\eta\in\kappa\right\rangle
. Let
C
be a club set containing no successor ordinals such that
h
is discontinuous on
C
, and let
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
be the increasing enumeration of
C\cup\left\{0\right\}
. For each
\xi\in\kappa
let
\lambda_{\xi}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\alpha_{\xi}}h(\alpha)
, where we have the convention that
\bigcup\varnothing=0
, then
\lambda_{\xi}<h(\alpha_{\xi})
for all
\xi\in\kappa
by discontinuity. Given
\alpha\in\kappa
, let
\xi
be such that
\alpha\in[\alpha_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi+1})
, which exists by
C
being club, and let
\varphi(\alpha)=\left\{f_{\eta}(\alpha)\mid\eta\in\lambda_{\xi}\right\}
. Since
h
is increasing,
|\varphi(\alpha)|\leq\lambda_{\xi}<h(\alpha_{\xi})\leq h(\alpha)
, thus
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
. Finally
h
is cofinal, thus
\left\langle\lambda_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
is cofinal, hence for every
\eta\in\kappa
we have
f_{\eta}\in^{*}\varphi
, showing that
\kappa<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.
∎We will once again see that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
is nontrivial in case (iii), but first let us consider
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.

In case (i) we have
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=2^{\kappa}
. The proof is a generalisation based on Lemmas 1.8, 1.10 and 1.11 from
[
GS93
]
, where the analogous theorem is proved for
{}^{\omega}\omega
.

Lemma 3.18

Suppose that there exists some
\lambda<\kappa
such that
D_{\lambda}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid h(\alpha)=\lambda\right\}
is cofinal in
\kappa
. Then
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=2^{\kappa}
.

We are only interested in finding a lower bound of
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
, thus by
3.15
we could restrict our attention to a cofinal subset of
\kappa
. We therefore assume without loss of generality that
D_{\lambda}=\kappa
, that is,
h(\alpha)=\lambda
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. To prove this lemma we assume furthermore without loss of generality that
b=h
. This suffices to prove the lemma, by
3.16
.

Let
b^{\prime}
be defined by
b^{\prime}(\alpha)=2^{|\alpha|}
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. We start with proving that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}(\in^{*})=2^{\kappa}
.

Let
\pi_{\alpha}:{}^{\alpha}2\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow}b^{\prime}(\alpha)
be an injection for every
\alpha\in\kappa
. For some arbitrary
g\in{}^{\kappa}2
, define
f_{g}\in\prod b^{\prime}
to be such that
f_{g}(\alpha)=\pi_{\alpha}(g\restriction\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. If
g,g^{\prime}\in{}^{\kappa}2
are distinct, then
g\restriction\alpha\neq g^{\prime}\restriction\alpha
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
, hence
f_{g}(\alpha)\neq f_{g^{\prime}}(\alpha)
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
. Therefore, if
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}
, then there are at most
\lambda
many functions
g\in{}^{\kappa}2
such that
f_{g}\in^{*}\varphi
. Since
{}^{\kappa}2
cannot be the union of less than
2^{\kappa}
sets of size
\lambda
, we see that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}(\in^{*})=2^{\kappa}
.

We will construct a Tukey connection
\mathscr{L}_{b^{\prime},h}\preceq\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
. Let
\left\langle W_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
be a partition of
\kappa
such that
|W_{\alpha}|=\lambda^{2^{|\alpha|}}
and let
\left\langle\Phi_{\xi}^{\alpha}\mid\xi\in W_{\alpha}\right\rangle
be an enumeration of all functions
2^{|\alpha|}\to\lambda
. We let
\rho_{-}:\prod b^{\prime}\to\prod b
send a function
f^{\prime}
to the function
f
defined as follows: for
\xi\in\kappa
, let
\alpha
be such that
\xi\in W_{\alpha}
, then we let
f(\xi)=\Phi_{\xi}^{\alpha}(f^{\prime}(\alpha))
. We let
\rho_{+}:\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}\to\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}
send a slalom
\varphi
to the slalom
\varphi^{\prime}
, where
\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)=\left\{\eta\in b^{\prime}(\alpha)\mid\forall\xi\in W_{\alpha}(\Phi_{\xi}^{\alpha}(\eta)\in\varphi(\xi))\right\}
. We show that
\varphi^{\prime}
is indeed a
(b^{\prime},h)
-slalom by proving that
|\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)|<h(\alpha)=\lambda
.

Assume towards contradiction that there exists a sequence
\left\langle\eta_{\beta}\mid\beta\in\lambda\right\rangle
of distinct elements of
\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)
. We define a function
\Phi:b^{\prime}(\alpha)\to\lambda
by sending
\eta_{\beta}\mapsto\beta
and
\eta\mapsto 0
if
\eta\neq\eta_{\beta}
for all
\beta\in\lambda
. Note that
\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)=b^{\prime}(\alpha)=2^{|\alpha|}
, hence there is
\xi\in W_{\alpha}
such that
\Phi=\Phi_{\xi}^{\alpha}
. Since
|\varphi(\xi)|<h(\xi)=\lambda
there is some
\beta\in\lambda\setminus\varphi(\xi)
, but then
\Phi(\eta_{\beta})=\Phi_{\xi}^{\alpha}(\eta_{\beta})\notin\varphi(\xi)
, which implies the contradictory
\eta_{\beta}\notin\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)
.

Finally we have to prove that
\rho_{-}
,
\rho_{+}
form a Tukey connection. If we assume that
f\in^{*}\varphi
, then
\Phi_{\xi}^{\alpha}(f^{\prime}(\alpha))\in\varphi(\xi)
for almost all
\xi\in\kappa
, where
\alpha
is such that
\xi\in W_{\alpha}
. This means that for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
we have
\Phi_{\xi}^{\alpha}(f^{\prime}(\alpha))\in\varphi(\xi)
for all
\xi\in W_{\alpha}
. Therefore, for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
we have
f^{\prime}(\alpha)\in\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)
, showing that
f^{\prime}\in^{*}\varphi^{\prime}
.
∎

The situation in case (ii) appears to be more complicated, and currently our best lower bound is given by the maximal size of almost disjoint families of functions. A family
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod b
is called
almost disjoint
if
f=^{\infty}g
implies that
f=g
for all
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 3.19

If
D_{\lambda}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid h(\alpha)=\lambda\right\}
is bounded for all
\lambda\in\kappa
and
h
is increasing and continuous on a stationary set and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod b
is an almost disjoint family, then
|\mathcal{A}|\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.

Since we assume
h\leq b
and
h
is cofinal,
b
is also cofinal. Note that there exists an almost disjoint family
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod b
with
|\mathcal{A}|=\kappa
: let
f_{\eta}\in\prod b
send
\alpha\mapsto\eta
if
\eta\in b(\alpha)
and
\alpha\mapsto 0
otherwise, then
\mathcal{A}=\left\{f_{\eta}\mid\eta\in\kappa\right\}
suffices. We will therefore assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{A}
is an almost disjoint family with
|\mathcal{A}|\geq\kappa
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, let
\lambda_{\varphi}
be minimal such that there is stationary
S_{\varphi}
with
|\varphi(\alpha)|=\lambda_{\varphi}
for all
\alpha\in S_{\varphi}
. Fix some arbitrary
A\subseteq\mathcal{A}
with
|A|=\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}
, enumerate
A
as
\left\langle f_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}\right\rangle
and define
\xi_{\alpha,\beta}=\min\left\{\xi\in\kappa\mid\forall\eta\in[\xi,\kappa)(f_{\alpha}(\eta)\neq f_{\beta}(\eta))\right\}
. Since
\xi_{\alpha,\beta}<\kappa
for all distinct
\alpha,\beta\in\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}
, and
\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}<\kappa
and
\kappa
is inaccessible, we see that
\xi=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}}\bigcup_{\beta\in\alpha}\xi_{\alpha,\beta}\in\kappa
. If
\eta\geq\xi
, then
f_{\alpha}(\eta)
is distinct for each
\alpha\in\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}
, thus
\left|\left\{f_{\alpha}(\eta)\mid\alpha\in\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}\right\}\right|=\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}
. For every
\eta\in S_{\varphi}\setminus\xi
there is
\alpha\in\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}
such that
f_{\alpha}(\eta)\notin\varphi(\eta)
, hence by the pigeonhole principle there exists
\alpha\in\lambda_{\varphi}^{+}
such that
f_{\alpha}\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{*}}}\varphi
.

Thus, we see that
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}=\left\{f\in\mathcal{A}\mid f\in^{*}\varphi\right\}
has
|\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}|\leq\lambda_{\varphi}
. If
\Phi\subseteq\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
is of minimal cardinality to witnesses
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
, then
\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}=\mathcal{A}
, and thus
\kappa\leq|\mathcal{A}|\leq|\Phi|\cdot\sup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}=|\Phi|
. For the last equality, note that
\sup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}\leq\kappa
and that this inequality would be strict if
|\Phi|<\kappa
.
∎

Note that an almost disjoint family
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod b
with
|\mathcal{A}|=2^{\kappa}
exists when there exists a continuous strictly increasing sequence
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
such that
2^{|\xi|}\leq|b(\alpha_{\xi})|
for all
\xi\in\kappa
. The construction for such a family is done by fixing bijections
\pi_{\xi}:{}^{\xi}2\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow}b(\alpha_{\xi})
and for any
f\in{}^{\kappa}2
considering the function
f^{\prime}\in\prod b
given by
f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto\pi_{\xi}(f\restriction\xi)
for each
\alpha\in[\alpha_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi+1})
, then
\left\{f^{\prime}\mid f\in{}^{\kappa}2\right\}
forms an almost disjoint family.

Even if
b
does not grow fast enough such that the above construction can be done, it is still possible to add an almost disjoint family of size
2^{\kappa}
with forcing. Let us focus on the case where
b=\mathsf{id}:\alpha\mapsto\alpha
is the identity function, and look at a forcing notion
\mathbb{AD}
that adds a
\lambda
-sized almost disjoint family of regressive functions, that is, elements of
\prod\mathsf{id}
.

Definition 3.20

The forcing notion
\mathbb{AD}
has the conditions
p:X_{p}\times\beta_{p}\to\kappa
such that
X_{p}\in[\lambda]^{<\kappa}
,
|X_{p}|\leq\beta_{p}\in\kappa
, and
p(\xi,\alpha)<\alpha
for all
\xi\in X_{p}
and
0<\alpha\in\beta_{p}
. Given
\xi\in X_{p}
, we write
p_{\xi}:\beta_{p}\to\kappa
for the (regressive) function
p_{\xi}:\alpha\mapsto p(\xi,\alpha)
. The ordering on
\mathbb{AD}
is given by
q\leq p
iff
p\subseteq q
(implicitly
X_{p}\subseteq X_{q}
and
\beta_{p}\leq\beta_{q}
), and
q_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq q_{\xi^{\prime}}(\alpha)
for any
\alpha\in\beta_{q}\setminus\beta_{p}
and distinct
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in X_{p}
.

Lemma 3.21

The set of all
p\in\mathbb{AD}
such that
(\xi,\alpha)\in\mathrm{dom}(p)
, is dense for any
\xi\in\lambda
and
\alpha\in\kappa
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{AD}
and
\beta_{p}\leq\alpha\in\kappa
, then we will first find
q\leq p
with
X_{q}=X_{p}
and
\beta_{q}=\alpha+1
. Fix an enumeration
\left\langle\xi_{\eta}\mid\eta<|X_{p}|\right\rangle
of
X_{p}
, then for any
\gamma\in[\beta_{p},\alpha]
note that
|X_{p}|\leq\beta_{p}\leq\gamma
, hence we can define
q_{\xi_{\eta}}(\gamma)=\eta<\gamma
, then
q\leq p
.

Next we show how to increase
X_{p}
. Let
p\in\mathbb{AD}
and
\mu<\kappa
, then by the above there exists
q\leq p
with
X_{q}=X_{p}
and
|X_{p}|+\mu\leq\beta_{q}
. If
X\in[\lambda\setminus X_{p}]^{\mu}
, we can find
r\leq q
with
X_{r}=X_{p}\cup X
and
\beta_{r}=\beta_{q}
simply by letting
r_{\xi}(\alpha)=0
for all
\alpha\in\beta_{q}
and
\xi\in X
.
∎

Lemma 3.22

If
G
is an
\mathbb{AD}
-generic filter over
\mathbf{V}
, and we define
f_{\xi}=\bigcup\left\{p_{\xi}\mid p\in G\land\xi\in X_{p})\right\}
, then
f_{\xi}\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is a regressive function and
\left\{f_{\xi}\mid\xi\in(\lambda)^{\mathbf{V}}\right\}
is almost disjoint.

It is clear from the definition of
\mathbb{AD}
and the above lemma that
f_{\xi}\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
and that
f_{\xi}
is regressive. If
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in(\lambda)^{\mathbf{V}}
are distinct, then let
p\in G
be such that
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in X_{p}
and let
\alpha\geq\beta_{p}
. For any
q\leq p
with
q\in G
and
\alpha\in\beta_{q}
we have
q_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq q_{\xi^{\prime}}(\alpha)
. By the above lemma there exist such
q\leq p
with
q\in G
, hence
f_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq f_{\xi^{\prime}}(\alpha)
for any
\alpha>\beta_{p}
. Therefore
f_{\xi}
and
f_{\xi^{\prime}}
are almost disjoint.
∎

Lemma 3.23

\mathbb{AD}
is
{<}\kappa
-closed and has the
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c..

First, let us prove
{<}\kappa
-closure. Let
\gamma\in\kappa
and let
\left\langle p^{\eta}\in\mathbb{AD}\mid\eta<\gamma\right\rangle
be a descending chain of conditions. It is clear that
X=\bigcup_{\eta\in\gamma}X_{p^{\eta}}\in[\lambda]^{<\kappa}
and
|X|\leq\beta=\bigcup_{\eta\in\gamma}\beta_{p^{\eta}}\in\kappa
, and that
p=\bigcup_{\eta\in\gamma}p^{\eta}:X\times\beta\to\kappa
and
p(\xi,\eta)<\eta
for all
(\xi,\eta)\in X\times\beta
. If
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in X_{p^{\eta}}
are distinct and
\alpha\in\beta\setminus\beta_{p^{\eta}}
, then there is
\zeta>\eta
such that
\alpha\in\beta_{p^{\zeta}}
. Since the chain of conditions is descending we have
p^{\zeta}\leq p^{\eta}
, which implies that
p_{\xi}(\alpha)=p^{\zeta}_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq p^{\zeta}_{\xi^{\prime}}(\alpha)=p_{\xi^{\prime}}(\alpha)
, so
p\leq p_{\eta}
.

As for
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c., suppose
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathbb{AD}
is a subset with
|\mathcal{B}|=\kappa^{+}
. We let
\mathcal{A}=\left\{\mathrm{dom}(p)\mid p\in\mathcal{B}\right\}
, then by
|X_{p}|<\kappa
and
\beta_{p}<\kappa
for each
p\in\mathcal{B}
we see that
\mathcal{A}
is a family of sets of cardinality
{<}\kappa
. Applying the
\Delta
-system lemma on
\mathcal{A}
, we know that there exists
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
such that
|\mathcal{B}_{0}|=\kappa^{+}
and some sets
X\in[\lambda]^{<\kappa}
and
\beta\in\kappa
such that
\mathrm{dom}(p)\cap\mathrm{dom}(p^{\prime})=X\times\beta
for all distinct
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
.

If
X=\varnothing
, then any
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
have some
q\leq p
and
q^{\prime}\leq p^{\prime}
such that
|X_{p}|+|X_{p^{\prime}}|\leq\beta_{q}=\beta_{q^{\prime}}
using
3.21
. Then
q\cup q^{\prime}\leq p
and
q\cup q^{\prime}\leq p^{\prime}
, thus
\mathcal{B}
is not an antichain.

On the other hand, if
X\neq\varnothing
, then
\beta=\beta_{p}=\beta_{p^{\prime}}
for all
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Since
|X\times\beta|<\kappa
and
\kappa^{<\kappa}=\kappa
, there exists
\mathcal{B}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}
with
|\mathcal{B}_{1}|=\kappa^{+}
such that
p\restriction(X\times\beta)=p^{\prime}\restriction(X\times\beta)
for all
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
. Once again, using
3.21
we can see that
p,p^{\prime}
are compatible, and thus
\mathcal{B}
is not an antichain.
∎

Corollary 3.24

\mathbb{AD}
preserves cardinals and cofinality and does not add any elements to
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Corollary 3.25

It is consistent that there exists an almost disjoint family
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod\mathsf{id}
of cardinality
2^{\kappa}=\lambda
for any
\lambda
with
\mathrm{cf}(\lambda)>\kappa
.

Starting with a model where
\mathbf{V}\vDash\text{``}\,2^{\kappa}=\lambda\,\text{''}
, let
G
be
\mathbb{AD}
-generic over
\mathbf{V}
, and let
\mathcal{F}=\left\{f_{\xi}\mid\xi\in(2^{\kappa})^{\mathbf{V}}\right\}
be the almost disjoint family described in
3.22
. An argument by counting names shows that
(2^{\kappa})^{\mathbf{V}}=(2^{\kappa})^{\mathbf{V}[G]}
.
∎

Although the
{<}\kappa
-closure implies that
\mathbb{AD}
preserves the inaccessibility of
\kappa
, the same cannot be said for stronger large cardinal assumptions on
\kappa
. Indeed, it is inconsistent that an almost disjoint family
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod\mathsf{id}
of size larger than
\kappa
exists for measurable cardinals, as was pointed out to me by Jing Zhang.

Theorem 3.26

If
\kappa
is measurable,
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is continuous and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod b
is almost disjoint, then
|\mathcal{A}|\leq\kappa
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\kappa)
be a
{<}\kappa
-complete nonprincipal normal ultrafilter on
\kappa
. Assume towards contradiction that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\prod b
is an almost disjoint family with
|\mathcal{A}|=\kappa^{+}
. Since
b
is continuous, the set of fixed points of
b
contains a club set, thus every
f\in\mathcal{A}
is regressive on a club set. Therefore there exists
X_{f}\in\mathcal{U}
such that
f
is regressive on
X_{f}
, and since
\mathcal{U}
is normal there exists
Y_{f}\in\mathcal{U}\restriction X_{f}
and
\gamma_{f}\in\kappa
such that
\mathrm{ran}(f\restriction Y_{f})=\left\{\gamma_{f}\right\}
. By the pigeonhole principle there exists
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and
\gamma\in\kappa
with
|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}|=|\mathcal{A}|
such that
\gamma_{f}=\gamma
for all
f\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Then for any distinct
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
we have
Y_{f}\cap Y_{f^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}
, contradicting that
f
and
f^{\prime}
are almost disjoint.
∎

In the end, we do not know whether the conditions of (ii) imply that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
is trivial, that is, the following question remains open:

Question 3.27

Is
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})<2^{\kappa}
consistent with
b
continuous on a club set?

Finally, in case (iii) we can prove that each of
\kappa^{+}<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})<2^{\kappa}
are consistent using a generalisation of localisation forcing. This mirrors what we saw for domination and unboundedness in
3.13
.

Definition 3.28

Let
C\subseteq\kappa
. We define
(b,h,C)
-Localisation forcing
