\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}^{b,h}_{\kappa}(C)
as the forcing notion with conditions
p=(s,F)
, where
s\in\mathrm{Loc}_{<\kappa}^{b,h}
is a function with domain
\gamma\in C
and
F\subseteq\prod b
with
|F|<h(\gamma)
. The ordering is given by
(t,G)\leq(s,F)
iff
s\subseteq t
and
F\subseteq G
and for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(t)\setminus\mathrm{dom}(s)
and
f\in F
we have
f(\alpha)\in t(\alpha)
.

It is clear from the definition, under the assumptions of case (iii), that
\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}^{b,h}_{\kappa}(C)
adds a generic element of
\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
that localises all elements of
\prod b
from the ground model.

Lemma 3.29

Let
C
be a club set such that
h
is discontinuous on
C
, then
\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
is strategically
{<}\kappa
-closed and
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c..

For strategic
{<}\kappa
-closure, at stage
\alpha
of
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C),p)
, let
\langle p_{\xi}=(s_{\xi},F_{\xi})\mid\xi\in\alpha\rangle
and
\langle p^{\prime}_{\xi}=(s_{\xi}^{\prime},F_{\xi}^{\prime})\mid\xi\in\alpha\rangle
be the sequences of previous moves by White and Black respectively. We will describe the winning strategy for White at stage
\alpha
.

If
\alpha=\beta+1
is successor, White will decide some
\gamma\in C
such that
h(\mathrm{dom}(s^{\prime}_{\beta}))<\gamma
and
\alpha<\gamma
. Since
|F_{\beta}^{\prime}|<h(\mathrm{dom}(s_{\beta}^{\prime}))
and
h
is increasing, we can define
s_{\alpha}\supseteq s_{\beta}^{\prime}
by letting
s_{\alpha}(\eta)=\{f(\eta)\mid f\in F_{\beta}^{\prime}\}
for each
\eta\in[\mathrm{dom}(s_{\beta}^{\prime}),\gamma)
and let
F_{\alpha}=F_{\beta}^{\prime}
.

We have to show that White can make a move at limit
\alpha
as well. We claim that
s_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}s_{\xi}
and
F_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}F_{\xi}
works. Clearly
\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})\in C
by
C
being club, and

\displaystyle|F_{\alpha}|=|\alpha|\cdot\textstyle\sup_{\xi\in\alpha}|F_{\xi}|\leq\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}h(\mathrm{dom}(s_{\xi}))<h(\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}\mathrm{dom}(s_{\xi}))=h(\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha}))

Here the strict inequality follows from
h
being discontinuous on
C
. Therefore,
(s_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})
is indeed a valid move for White, implying that White has a winning strategy.

For
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c., note that for any
(s,F),(s,G)\in\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
with
\mathrm{dom}(s)=\gamma
we have
|F|<h(\gamma)
and
|G|<h(\gamma)
, hence since
h(\gamma)
is infinite also
|F\cup G|<h(\gamma)
. Therefore
(s,F\cup G)
is a condition below both
(s,F)
and
(s,G)
. Thus if
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
is an antichain and
(s,F),(t,G)\in\mathcal{A}
are distinct, then we must have
s\neq t
, hence
|\mathcal{A}|\leq\kappa
.
∎

Corollary 3.30

\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}^{b,h}_{\kappa}(C)
preserves cardinals and cofinality and does not any elements to
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Theorem 3.31

Let
C
be a club set such that
h
is discontinuous on
C
, then it is consistent that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})>\kappa^{+}
and that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})<2^{\kappa}
.

Analogous to
3.13
, with
\mathbb{L}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
taking the role of
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
.
∎

3.3. Antilocalisation & Anti-avoidance

We can give similar results for
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
to what we discussed in the previous sections. Firstly, we make three observations, without proof, analogous to
3.14
,
3.15
and
3.16
. As a consequence we will once again assume that
h\leq b
is always the case.

Lemma 3.32

b<^{\infty}h
if and only if
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=1
.

If
b<^{\infty}h
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
is undefined.

Lemma 3.33

If
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
is a strictly increasing sequence and
b^{\prime}:\xi\mapsto b(\alpha_{\xi})
and
h^{\prime}:\xi\mapsto h(\alpha_{\xi})
, then
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

Lemma 3.34

Let
h\geq^{*}h^{\prime}
and
b\leq^{*}b^{\prime}
, then there exist Tukey connections
{\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}}\preceq{\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}}
.

A comprehensive overview of the trivial values for these cardinals in the classical context has been given by Cardona and Mejía in section 3 of
[
CM19
]
. However, the classical characterisation uses a substantial amount of finite arithmetic and thus appears quite different from the characterisation of the trivial values of
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
, given below. We once again have three cases, making this similar to
3.3
and
3.17
.

Lemma 3.35

For each
\lambda<\kappa
define

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid b(\alpha)\leq\lambda\right\}\ \cup\ \left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)\text{ and }\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))\leq\lambda\right\}.

(i)

If there exists a least cardinal
\lambda<\kappa
such that
D_{\lambda}
is cofinal in
\kappa
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\lambda
.

(ii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all
\lambda<\kappa
and there is a stationary set
S
such that

(a)

b
is continuous on
S
,
or

(b)

for each
\xi\in S
there exists
\alpha_{\xi}\geq\xi
with
h(\alpha_{\xi})=b(\alpha_{\xi})
and
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha_{\xi}))\leq\xi
,

then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\kappa
.

(iii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all
\lambda<\kappa
and there is a club set
C
such that

(a)

b
is discontinuous on C,
and

(b)

for each
\xi\in C
and
\alpha\geq\xi
, if
h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
, then
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))>\xi
,

then
\kappa^{+}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
.

(i) Assume that
\lambda
is minimal such that
D_{\lambda}
is cofinal. Let
D_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid b(\alpha)=\lambda\right\}
and
D_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)\text{ and }\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))=\lambda\right\}
. Note that at least one of
D_{\lambda}^{\prime}
or
D_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}
is cofinal. We will assume without loss of generality that
D_{\lambda}=D^{\prime}_{\lambda}\cup D^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}
.

First, we will show that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\lambda
.

If
\alpha\in D_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}
, then
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))=\lambda
, thus we can find a continuous sequence
\langle\beta_{\xi}^{\alpha}\mid\xi<\lambda\rangle
that is cofinal in
b(\alpha)
. In the other case where
\alpha\in D_{\lambda}^{\prime}
, let
\beta_{\xi}^{\alpha}=\xi
for all
\xi<\lambda=b(\alpha)
.

Since
|[\beta_{\xi}^{\alpha},\beta_{\xi+1}^{\alpha})|<h(\alpha)\leq b(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in D_{\lambda}
, we can pick some
\varphi_{\xi}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
for each
\xi<\lambda
such that
\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)=[\beta_{\xi}^{\alpha},\beta_{\xi+1}^{\alpha})
for all
\alpha\in D_{\lambda}
. For
f\in\prod b
, let
F_{f}:D_{\lambda}\to\lambda
map
\alpha\in D_{\lambda}
to the
\xi\in\lambda
such that
f(\alpha)\in\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)
, then by the pigeonhole principle
F_{f}^{-1}(\xi)
is cofinal for some
\xi\in\lambda
, and thus for this
\xi
we have
f\in^{\infty}\varphi_{\xi}
. Therefore
\left\{\varphi_{\xi}\mid\xi<\lambda\right\}
witnesses that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\lambda
.

Next we show that
\lambda\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
. Let
\left\{\varphi_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\mu\right\}\subseteq\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
for some
\mu<\lambda
.

If
D_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is cofinal, then we must have
b(\alpha)\geq\lambda
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
by minimality of
\lambda
. Suppose that
\lambda\leq b(\alpha)
and
\bigcup_{\xi<\mu}\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
, then
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))\leq\mu
, and furthermore for every
\nu<b(\alpha)
there is some
\xi\in\mu
such that
\nu\leq|\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)|
, implying that
h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
. But then
\alpha\in D^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
, which is bounded by minimality of
\lambda
. Therefore
\bigcup_{\xi<\mu}\varphi(\alpha)\neq b(\alpha)
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
.

If
D_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is bounded and
D_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}
is cofinal, then for almost all
\alpha\notin D_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}
we must have
h(\alpha)\neq b(\alpha)
or
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))>\lambda
. Hence we have
\max\left\{h(\alpha),\lambda\right\}<b(\alpha)
or
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))\geq\lambda
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
. If
\max\left\{h(\alpha),\lambda\right\}<b(\alpha)
, then also
\mu\cdot h(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
, thus
\bigcup_{\xi<\mu}\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq b(\alpha)
. On the other hand if
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))\geq\lambda
, then clearly
\bigcup_{\xi<\mu}\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq b(\alpha)
as well.

In either case we find
f\in\prod b
such that
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi_{\xi}
for all
\xi<\mu
, and consequently
\mu<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
.

(ii) We first prove that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\kappa
in case (a), then in case (b), and then we show the reverse direction, that
\kappa\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
.

(a) Assume
b
is increasing and continuous on stationary
S\subseteq\kappa
. Then the set of fixed points
S^{\prime}=\left\{\alpha\in S\mid b(\alpha)=\alpha\right\}
is also stationary.

For each
\eta\in\kappa
let
\varphi_{\eta}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
be defined as
\varphi_{\eta}(\xi)=\left\{\eta\right\}
if
\eta<b(\xi)
and arbitrary otherwise. If
f\in\prod b
, then
f(\xi)<b(\xi)
for every
\xi\in\kappa
, therefore
f
is regressive on
S^{\prime}
. By Fodor’s lemma then there exists stationary
S^{\prime\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
such that
f\restriction S^{\prime\prime}
is constant. Let
\eta
be such that
f(\xi)=\eta
for all
\eta\in S^{\prime\prime}
, then clearly
f\in^{\infty}\varphi_{\eta}
. Hence
\left\{\varphi_{\eta}\mid\eta<\kappa\right\}
witnesses
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\kappa
.

(b) Assume
S
is stationary and for each
\xi\in S
let
\alpha_{\xi}\geq\xi
be such that
h(\alpha_{\xi})=b(\alpha_{\xi})
and
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha_{\xi}))\leq\xi
. We define for each
\eta\in\kappa
a slalom
\varphi_{\eta}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
in such a way that for each
\xi\in S
we have
\bigcup_{\eta<\xi}\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha_{\xi})=b(\alpha_{\xi})
, which is possible since
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha_{\xi}))\leq\xi
and
h(\alpha_{\xi})=b(\alpha_{\xi})
.

If
f\in\prod b
, then for each
\xi\in S
we define
f^{\prime}(\xi)
to be the least
\eta<\xi
such that
f(\alpha_{\xi})\in\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha_{\xi})
. Now
f^{\prime}
is regressive on
S
, so by Fodor’s lemma there exists stationary
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
such that
f^{\prime}\restriction S^{\prime}
is constant, say with value
\eta
. Then
f(\alpha_{\xi})\in\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha_{\xi})
for each
\xi\in S^{\prime}
, thus
f\in^{\infty}\varphi_{\eta}
. Hence
\left\{\varphi_{\eta}\mid\eta<\kappa\right\}
witnesses
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\kappa
.

Finally for the reverse direction, let
\lambda<\kappa
and
\left\{\varphi_{\xi}\mid\xi<\lambda\right\}\subseteq\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
. Since
D_{\lambda}
is bounded, we can find
\alpha_{0}\in\kappa
such that for every
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
we have
\lambda<b(\alpha)
and either
h(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
or
\lambda<\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))
. If
h(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
, then
\lambda\cdot h(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
, meaning
\bigcup_{\xi<\lambda}\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq b(\alpha)
. On the other hand, if
\lambda<\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))
, then by
|\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)|<h(\alpha)\leq b(\alpha)
we see that once again
\bigcup_{\xi<\lambda}\varphi_{\xi}(\alpha)\neq b(\alpha)
. Hence we can construct
f\in\prod b
such that
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi_{\xi}
for all
\xi<\lambda
, which proves that
\kappa\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
.

(iii) Let
C
be a club set with the properties mentioned in (iii) and let
\left\{\varphi_{\eta}\mid\eta\in\kappa\right\}\subseteq\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
. We can enumerate
C
increasingly as
\left\langle\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
, then every
\alpha\in\kappa
has some
\xi\in\kappa
such that
\alpha\in[\alpha_{\xi},\alpha_{\xi+1})
. Note that
|\bigcup_{\eta\in\xi}\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha)|\leq|\xi|\cdot\sup_{\eta\in\xi}|\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha)|
. Since
b
is increasing and discontinuous on
C
and
\xi\leq\alpha_{\xi}\leq\alpha
, we see that
\xi<b(\alpha_{\xi})\leq b(\alpha)
. If
h(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
, then it is clear that
\sup_{\eta\in\xi}|\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha)|<b(\alpha)
as well. Else, if
h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
, then by the properties of
C
we see that
\mathrm{cf}(b(\alpha))>\alpha_{\xi}\geq\xi
, thus it also follows that
\sup_{\eta\in\xi}|\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha)|<b(\alpha)
. We can therefore conclude that
|\bigcup_{\eta\in\xi}\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha)|<b(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
, and thus we can define
f(\alpha)
to be some value in
b(\alpha)
that lies outside of
\varphi_{\eta}(\alpha)
for each
\eta<\xi
. Then clearly
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi_{\eta}
for all
\eta\in\kappa
.
∎

As with domination and localisation, it is not known whether cases (i) and (ii) of
3.35
imply that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=2^{\kappa}
. If we consider the constant function
\overline{2}
, then we see that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,\overline{2}}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
by
3.34
. Since
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,\overline{2}}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
(see
3.41
), we will discuss the cases (i) and (ii) in the next section. Let us mention here, that if, by exception, we allow that
b(\alpha)
is finite for cofinally many
\alpha
, then we may use a connection with localisation cardinals to prove that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=2^{\kappa}
(see
3.44
). We will also give a partial answer to case (i) in the form of the following lemma:

Lemma 3.36

If
\lambda
is regular and
D=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid b(\alpha)=h(\alpha)=\lambda\right\}
is cofinal, then
\mathscr{D}_{\overline{\lambda}}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

Enumerate
D
as
\left\{\alpha_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\}
. We need
\rho_{-}:{}^{\kappa}\lambda\to\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
and
\rho_{+}:\prod b\to{}^{\kappa}\lambda
.

Given
g\in{}^{\kappa}\lambda
, let
\rho_{-}(g)\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
be such that
\rho_{-}(g):\alpha_{\xi}\mapsto g(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\kappa
. Given
f\in\prod b
, let
\rho_{+}(f):\xi\mapsto f(\alpha_{\xi})
for each
\xi\in\kappa
. Since
\alpha_{\xi}\in D
for each
\xi\in\kappa
, we have
h(\alpha_{\xi})=b(\alpha_{\xi})=\lambda
, thus
\rho_{-}
and
\rho_{+}
are well-defined. If
\varphi=\rho_{-}(g)
and
f^{\prime}=\rho_{+}(f)
, and
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi
, then it is easy to see that
g\leq^{*}f^{\prime}
, so this is a Tukey connection.
∎

Since we already know that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\lambda}}(\leq^{*})=2^{\kappa}
from
3.5
, the assumptions of the above lemma imply that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=2^{\kappa}
.

Definition 3.37

Let
C\subseteq\kappa
. We define
(b,h,C)
-antilocalisation forcing

\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
as the forcing with conditions
p=(s,\Phi)
where
s\in\prod_{<\kappa}b
is a function with domain
\gamma\in C
and
\Phi\subseteq\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
with
|\Phi|\leq\gamma
. The ordering is given by
(t,\Psi)\leq(s,\Phi)
if
s\subseteq t
and
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
and for each
\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(t)\setminus\mathrm{dom}(s)
and
\varphi\in\Phi
we have
t(\alpha)\notin\varphi(\alpha)
.

It is clear from the definition, under the assumptions of case (iii), that
\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
adds a generic function in
\prod b
that is antilocalised by every ground model
(b,h)
-slalom.

Lemma 3.38

Let
C
be a club set with such that the assumptions of (iii) from
3.35
are satisfied. Then
\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
is strategically
{<}\kappa
-closed and
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c..

For strategic
{<}\kappa
-closure, at stage
\alpha
of
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C),p)
, let
\langle p_{\xi}=(s_{\xi},\Phi_{\xi})\mid\xi\in\alpha\rangle
and
\langle p^{\prime}_{\xi}=(s_{\xi}^{\prime},\Phi_{\xi}^{\prime})\mid\xi\in\alpha\rangle
be the sequences of previous moves by White and Black respectively. We will describe the winning strategy for White at stage
\alpha
.

If
\alpha=\beta+1
is successor, White will decide some
\gamma\in C
such that
\alpha\leq\gamma
. Let
\mathrm{dom}(s_{\beta}^{\prime})=\gamma^{\prime}\in C
, then if
\xi\in[\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)
we have
|\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}\varphi(\xi)|\leq|\gamma^{\prime}|\cdot\sup_{\varphi\in\Phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}|\varphi(\xi)|<b(\xi)
through the same reasoning as in the proof of
3.35
(iii). Therefore, we can find some value
s_{\alpha}(\xi)\in b(\xi)\setminus\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Phi_{\beta}^{\prime}}\varphi(\xi)
to define
s_{\alpha}
with
\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})=\gamma
, and let
\Phi_{\alpha}=\Phi_{\beta}^{\prime}
.

We have to show that White can make a move at limit
\alpha
as well. We claim that
s_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}s_{\xi}
and
\Phi_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}\Phi_{\xi}
works. Clearly
\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})\in C
by
C
being club, and

\displaystyle|\Phi_{\alpha}|\leq|\alpha|\cdot\textstyle\sup_{\xi\in\alpha}|\Phi_{\xi}|=|\alpha|\cdot\bigcup_{\xi\in\alpha}\mathrm{dom}(s_{\xi})\leq|\alpha|\leq\mathrm{dom}(s_{\alpha})

Therefore,
(s_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})
is indeed a valid move for White, so White has a winning strategy.

For
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c., note that for any
(s,\Phi),(s,\Psi)\in\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
with
\mathrm{dom}(s)=\gamma
we have
|\Phi|\leq\gamma
and
|\Psi|\leq\gamma
. We may assume without loss of generality that
\gamma
is infinite, then also
|\Phi\cup\Psi|\leq\gamma
, hence
(s,\Phi\cup\Psi)
is a condition below both
(s,\Phi)
and
(s,\Psi)
. Thus if
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
is an antichain and
(s,\Phi),(t,\Psi)\in\mathcal{A}
are distinct, then we must have
s\neq t
, hence
|\mathcal{A}|\leq\kappa
.
∎

Corollary 3.39

\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
preserves cardinals and cofinality and does not add any elements to
{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
.

Theorem 3.40

Let
C
be a club set and
b,h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be such that the assumptions of (iii) from
3.35
are satisfied, then it is consistent that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})>\kappa^{+}
and that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})<2^{\kappa}
.

Analogous to
3.13
, with
\mathbb{AL}\mathbbm{oc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(C)
taking the role of
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
.
∎

3.4. Eventual Difference & Cofinal Equality

Eventual difference is a special case of antilocalisation, where the function
h
that determines the size of the sets in the slaloms is as small as possible:

Lemma 3.41

\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}\equiv\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,\overline{2}}
.

Any slalom
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,\overline{2}}
is a sequence of singletons, and thus we can define
f_{\varphi}\in\prod b
such that
\left\{f_{\varphi}(\alpha)\right\}=\varphi(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. It follows that
g\in^{\infty}\varphi
if and only if
g=^{\infty}f_{\varphi}
.
∎

It follows that
3.35
immediately gives us the following characterisation of the trivial values of
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
, since we can ignore the case where
h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
if we let
h=\overline{2}
.

Corollary 3.42

(cf.
3.35
)

For each
\lambda<\kappa
define
D_{\lambda}=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid b(\alpha)\leq\lambda\right\}
.

(i)

If there exists a least cardinal
\lambda<\kappa
such that
D_{\lambda}
is cofinal in
\kappa
, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\lambda
.

(ii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all
\lambda<\kappa
and
b
is continuous on a stationary set, then
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\kappa
.

(iii)

If
D_{\lambda}
is bounded for all
\lambda<\kappa
and
b
is discontinuous on a club set, then
\kappa^{+}\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
.

It is equally immediate that the forcing construction from
3.40
gives us a consistency result in case (iii) of
3.42
.

Corollary 3.43

(Cf.
3.40
)

Let
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be such that the assumptions of (iii) from
3.42
are satisfied, then it is consistent that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})>\kappa^{+}
and that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})<2^{\kappa}
.

We can use
3.18
and a special connection between localisation and antilocalisation for functions with finite values to prove the following lemma, giving a condition for which
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
are trivial. Note that this is significantly weaker than the assumption in case (i), since
b
is not only bounded on a cofinal set, but even finite cofinally often.

Lemma 3.44

If
D=\left\{\alpha\in\kappa\mid b(\alpha)<\omega\right\}
is cofinal, then
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=2^{\kappa}
.

We want to give a lower bound to
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
, thus by
3.34
we may assume that
h=\overline{2}
, and moreover by
3.33
and
D
being cofinal, we may assume that
b=\overline{n}
for some
n\in\omega
.

Note that
\mathscr{L}_{\overline{n},\overline{n}}\equiv\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{\overline{n},\overline{2}}
. Namely, if
f\in\prod b
, let
\varphi_{f}:\alpha\mapsto n\setminus\left\{f(\alpha)\right\}
, then
\varphi_{f}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{\overline{n},\overline{n}}
, and on the other hand, if
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{\overline{n},\overline{2}}
, then let
f_{\varphi}
be the unique function such that
\varphi(\alpha)=\left\{f_{\varphi}(\alpha)\right\}
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. It is clear that
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi
if and only if
f_{\varphi}\in^{*}\varphi_{f}
.

It follows that
2^{\kappa}=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\overline{n},\overline{n}}(\in^{*})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{\overline{n}}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,\overline{2}}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
if
D
is cofinal. Here the first equality is given by
3.18
.
∎

The above argument is able to relate eventual difference to localisation by removing a single element from a finite set, which changes its cardinality. Naturally, removing a single element from an infinite set does not change the cardinality of the infinite set, thus the technique from this proof does not give us insight on how to prove
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=2^{\kappa}
if
b
is only bounded on a cofinal set by an infinite value.

It is equally unclear whether
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=2^{\kappa}
in case (ii) of
3.42
.

Question 3.45

Is
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})<2^{\kappa}
consistent in case (i) or (ii) of
3.42
?

4. More Relations Between Cardinals

In this section we will give an overview of some additional relations between the cardinals we have mentioned so far.

4.1. Relations for Fixed Parameters

We do this by defining Tukey connections. The following theorem gives an overview of relations between the cardinals we have discussed so far, for a fixed pair of parameters
b,h
such that the relevant cardinals are nontrivial.

Theorem 4.1

Let
b,h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be such that the conditions of cases (iii) of
3.35
,
3.3
and
3.17
are satisfied, then the following Tukey connections exist, where the relations marked by
†
require the additional assumption that
h\leq^{*}\mathrm{cf}(b)
.

\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
\mathscr{D}_{b}
\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}
\mathscr{D}^{\bot}_{b}
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}^{\bot}_{b,h}
\preceq^{\dagger}
\preceq^{\dagger}
{\preceq}^{\dagger}
\preceq
\preceq
\preceq
\preceq

The relation between eventual difference and antilocalisation was already established with
3.41
, and combined with
3.34
we get
\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

It is also easy to see for
f,g\in\prod b
, that if
g\leq^{*}f
, then
f+1\mathbin{\cancel{\leq^{*}}}g
and
g\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}f+1
. This implies that there are Tukey connections
\mathscr{D}^{\bot}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
and
\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
.

Next, for
f\in\prod b
and
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, we have that
f\in^{*}\varphi
implies
f\in^{\infty}\varphi
, and thus we get a Tukey connection
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}^{\bot}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

Finally we will show that
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
as long as we assume that
h\leq^{*}\mathrm{cf}(b)
. Note that this also implies
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}^{\bot}_{b,h}\succeq\mathscr{D}_{b}^{\bot}
by duality.

We define
\rho_{-}^{0}:\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}\to\prod b
and
\rho_{+}^{0}:\prod b\to\prod b
as a Tukey connection for
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
, and
\rho_{-}^{1}:\prod b\to\prod b
and
\rho_{+}^{1}:\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}\to\prod b
as a Tukey connection for
\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

We will have
\rho_{-}^{0}=\rho_{+}^{1}
sending
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
to
g\in\prod b
where
g(\alpha)=\sup(\varphi(\alpha))
if
\sup(\varphi(\alpha))<b(\alpha)
and arbitrary otherwise. Since
h\leq^{*}\mathrm{cf}(b)
, we see that
\varphi(\alpha)
is not cofinal in
b(\alpha)
for almost all
\alpha
, so
g(\alpha)=\sup(\varphi(\alpha))
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
. We let
\rho_{-}^{1}
be the identity function, and
\rho_{+}^{0}:f\mapsto f+1
.

If
f\in\prod b
and
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, let
g=\rho_{-}^{0}(\varphi)=\rho_{+}^{1}(\varphi)
and
f+1=\rho_{+}^{0}(f)
. Then
f\in^{*}\varphi
implies
f(\alpha)\leq\sup(\varphi(\alpha))=g(\alpha)
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
, hence
f\leq^{*}g
. On the other hand, if
g\leq^{*}f
, then
g<^{*}f+1
, hence
\sup(\varphi(\alpha))<f(\alpha)+1
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
, implying
f+1\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi
.
∎

In summary, we can draw the cardinal characteristics related to these relational systems in the diagram below, where the dashed lines require that
h\leq^{*}\mathrm{cf}(b)
. Note that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
follow from
3.41
and
3.34
and
2.11
.

\kappa^{+}
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
2^{\kappa}

Figure 1:
Diagram of the relations between cardinals on bounded spaces

We saw that
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
if
h\leq^{*}\mathrm{cf}(b)
, and in
3.6
we showed that reversely
\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{D}_{b}
if
h=^{*}b
. Under the same assumption, we can also prove that
\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
. Therefore, the dashed arrows in the above diagram collapse to become equalities in the case where
b=^{*}\mathrm{cf}(b)=^{*}h
.

Theorem 4.2

If
h=^{*}b
, then
\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

We define
\rho_{-}:\prod b\to\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
and
\rho_{+}:\prod b\to\prod b
as a Tukey connection for
\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

We will have
\rho_{-}
sending
g\in\prod b
to
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
where
\varphi(\alpha)=g(\alpha)
whenever
h(\alpha)=b(\alpha)
and arbitrary otherwise. This is well-defined, since
|g(\alpha)|<b(\alpha)=h(\alpha)
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
. Let
\rho_{+}
be the identity function.

If
f,g\in\prod b
, let
\varphi=\rho_{-}(g)
. Then
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi
implies
f(\alpha)\notin\varphi(\alpha)=g(\alpha)
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
, hence
g\leq^{*}f
.
∎

The relation
\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
can also be reversed for certain choices of
h
and
b
, by the following theorem.

Theorem 4.3

Let
b
be increasing (not strictly) and
\left\langle I_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\kappa\right\rangle
be an interval partition of
\kappa
with
|I_{\alpha}|=h(\alpha)
for each
\alpha\in\kappa
such that
b(\alpha)=b(\xi)=b(\alpha)^{h(\alpha)}
for all
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
and
\alpha\in\kappa
, then
\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}\equiv\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
.

We already know
\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}\preceq\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}
from
4.1
, thus we show that
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}
. We do this by giving a Tukey connection
\rho_{-}:\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}\to\prod b
and
\rho_{+}:\prod b\to\prod b
. For each
\alpha\in\kappa
let
\pi_{\alpha}:b(\alpha)\to{}^{I_{\alpha}}b(\alpha)
be a bijection, which exist by
b(\alpha)^{h(\alpha)}=b(\alpha)
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, let
\lambda_{\alpha}=|\varphi(\alpha)|
and enumerate each
\varphi(\alpha)=\{x^{\alpha}_{\xi}\in b(\alpha)\mid\xi\in\lambda_{\alpha}\}
. We define
g_{\xi}^{\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}(x^{\alpha}_{\xi})\in{}^{I_{\alpha}}b(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
and
\xi\in\lambda_{\alpha}
. Fix
\iota_{\alpha}=\min(I_{\alpha})
, then for every
\xi\in\lambda_{\alpha}
we see that
\iota_{\alpha}+\xi\in I_{\alpha}
, since
\lambda_{\alpha}<h(\alpha)=|I_{\alpha}|
. Let
g\in\prod b
be a function such that
g(\iota_{\alpha}+\xi)=g^{\alpha}_{\xi}(\iota_{\alpha}+\xi)
for each
\alpha\in\kappa
and
\xi\in\lambda_{\alpha}
. This is well defined, because
b(\alpha)=b(\xi)
for all
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
. We let
\rho_{-}(\varphi)=g
.

We define
\rho_{+}(f)\in\prod b
sending
\alpha\mapsto\pi_{\alpha}^{-1}(f\restriction I_{\alpha})
.

Now suppose that
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
and
f\in\prod b
and let
g=\rho_{-}(\varphi)
and
f^{\prime}=\rho_{+}(f)
. Suppose that
f^{\prime}\in^{\infty}\varphi
and
\alpha_{0}\in\kappa
, then there is
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
such that
f^{\prime}(\alpha)\in\varphi(\alpha)
, so pick
\xi\in\lambda_{\alpha}
such that
x^{\alpha}_{\xi}=f^{\prime}(\alpha)
. Then we see

\displaystyle g^{\alpha}_{\xi}=\pi_{\alpha}(x_{\xi}^{\alpha})=\pi_{\alpha}(f^{\prime}(\alpha))=\pi_{\alpha}(\pi^{-1}_{\alpha}(f\restriction I_{\alpha}))=f\restriction I_{\alpha}.

In particular
f(\iota_{\alpha}+\xi)=g_{\xi}^{\alpha}(\iota_{\alpha}+\xi)=g(\iota_{\alpha}+\xi)
, and since
\iota_{\alpha}+\xi>\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{0}
was arbitrary, it follows that
f=^{\infty}g
.
∎

Note that the proof does not require the assumption that
b(\alpha)<\kappa
for
\alpha\in\kappa
. Indeed, the above theorem holds even if we let
b:\kappa\to\left\{\kappa\right\}
, since
\kappa
is inaccessible. It follows that the cardinal characteristics
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
from the (unbounded) generalised Baire space do not depend on the choice of
h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, and can be related to the
\kappa
-meagre ideal as a consequence of
2.11
.

Corollary 4.4

\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
for any choice of
h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

We will conclude this subsection with a relationship between antilocalisation and localisation for different parameters.

Theorem 4.5

Let
h\cdot h^{\prime}<^{*}b
and
b^{g}\leq^{*}b^{\prime}
for all
g<h
, then there exists a Tukey connection
{\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}}\preceq{\mathscr{L}_{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}}
.

Let
\alpha_{0}\in\kappa
be large enough such that
h(\alpha)\cdot h^{\prime}(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
and
b(\alpha)^{<h(\alpha)}\leq b^{\prime}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
. For each
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, we fix an injection
\iota_{\alpha}:[b(\alpha)]^{<h(\alpha)}\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow}b^{\prime}(\alpha)
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, let
\rho_{-}(\varphi)=f^{\prime}\in\prod b^{\prime}
map
\alpha\mapsto\iota_{\alpha}(\varphi(\alpha))
for any
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, and arbitrary otherwise. Given
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}
, let
\rho_{+}(\varphi)=f\in\prod b
have
f(\alpha)\in b(\alpha)\setminus\bigcup_{\xi\in\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)\cap\mathrm{ran}(\iota_{\alpha})}\iota_{\alpha}^{-1}(\xi)
for any
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, and arbitrary otherwise. Note that this is well-defined, since
|\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)|<h^{\prime}(\alpha)
and
|\iota_{\alpha}^{-1}(\xi)|<h(\alpha)
, therefore
|\bigcup_{\xi\in\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)\cap\mathrm{ran}(\iota_{\alpha})}\iota_{\alpha}^{-1}(\xi)|\leq h(\alpha)\cdot h^{\prime}(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
.

If
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
and
f^{\prime}(\alpha)\in\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)
, then
\varphi(\alpha)=\iota_{\alpha}^{-1}(f^{\prime}(\alpha))\subseteq\bigcup_{\xi\in\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)\cap\mathrm{ran}(\iota_{\alpha})}\iota_{\alpha}^{-1}(\xi)
, so
f(\alpha)\notin\varphi(\alpha)
. Therefore
f^{\prime}\in^{*}\varphi^{\prime}
implies
f\mathrel{\cancel{\in^{\infty}}}\varphi
.
∎

4.2. Infima and Suprema

Remember that we have the following monotonicity results from
3.16
and
3.34
. Let
b\leq b^{\prime}
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

This motivates the definition of the following cardinal characteristics.

Definition 4.6

Given
h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, let:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})\mid b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})

\displaystyle=\sup\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})\mid b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\mid b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle=\sup\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\mid b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\mid b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle=\sup\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\mid b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\}

Note that for
\in^{*}
we take infima over the
\mathfrak{b}
-side cardinals, while for
\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}}
and
\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}
we take infima over the
\mathfrak{d}
-side cardinals, and vice versa for the suprema. Note also that
\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{\overline{2}}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{\overline{2}}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
by
3.41
.

In [CM19] it is proved that the parameter
h
does not matter if we work in
{}^{\omega}\omega
. This is based on two claims which we repeat below.

Fact 4.7

For any
b,h,h^{\prime}\in{}^{\omega}\omega
such that
h\leq h^{\prime}
there exists
b^{\prime}\in{}^{\omega}\omega
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\in^{*})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}^{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(\in^{*})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}(\in^{*})\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
.

Fact 4.8

For any
b^{\prime},h\in{}^{\omega}\omega
such that
\overline{2}\leq h
there exists
b\in{}^{\omega}\omega
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}^{b^{\prime}}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{b^{\prime}}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
.

In the generalised context,
4.7
can only be partially generalised, and indeed we will see that differences in the parameter
h
can lead to consistently different cardinals. Meanwhile
4.8
is completely generalisable and we will look at this first.
One could compare the next lemma to
4.3
.

Lemma 4.9

For any
b^{\prime},h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
\overline{2}\leq h
there exists
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
\mathscr{A}\!\!\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b^{\prime}}
.

Let
\left\langle I_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
be the (unique) interval partition of
\kappa
such that
I_{\alpha}\subseteq\min(I_{\beta})
for
\alpha<\beta
and
|I_{\alpha}|=h(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. We define
b(\alpha)=\left|\prod_{\xi\in I_{\alpha}}b^{\prime}(\xi)\right|
and let
\pi_{\alpha}:b(\alpha)\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow}\prod_{\xi\in I_{\alpha}}b^{\prime}(\xi)
be a bijection for each
\alpha\in\kappa
.

For each
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, let
\rho_{-}(\varphi)=g_{\varphi}\in\prod b^{\prime}
be defined as follows. For each
\alpha\in\kappa
and we take some surjection
\iota_{\alpha}^{\varphi}:I_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\varphi(\alpha)
. Given
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
, let
\beta=\iota_{\alpha}^{\varphi}(\xi)\in\varphi(\alpha)
, then we define
g_{\varphi}(\xi)=\pi_{\alpha}(\beta)(\xi)
.

For
f^{\prime}\in\prod b^{\prime}
, we let
\rho_{+}(f^{\prime})=f\in\prod b
be given by
f(\alpha)=\pi_{\alpha}^{-1}(f^{\prime}\restriction I_{\alpha})
.

Now
(\rho_{-},\rho_{+})
forms a Tukey connection. If
\rho_{+}(f^{\prime})=f\in^{\infty}\varphi
, let
\alpha\in\kappa
be arbitrarily large such that
f(\alpha)\in\varphi(\alpha)
. Take
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
such that
f(\alpha)=\iota_{\alpha}^{\varphi}(\xi)
, then
g_{\varphi}(\xi)=\pi_{\alpha}(f(\alpha))(\xi)=f^{\prime}(\xi)
, thus since
\alpha
is arbitrarily large, we see that
f^{\prime}=^{\infty}g_{\varphi}
.
∎

Corollary 4.10

\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
for each
h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

By
3.34
we see that
\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\geq\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
.

By
4.9
, for any
b^{\prime}\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
we can find some
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},\overline{2}}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
, thus
\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\geq\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
. Similar for
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
.
∎

An equality between
\mathfrak{inf}_{\omega}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})
was first found by Rothberger
[
Rot41
]
. Miller
[
Mil81
]
used this result to remark that
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{inf}_{\omega}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}
. One could also show that
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{sup}_{\omega}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}
. We show that each of these three claims generalises.

Instead of proving
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})=\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
directly, we show one direction by use of a Tukey connection. Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\left\{(g,b)\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa\times{}^{\kappa}\kappa\mid g<b\right\}
and
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\left\{\tau:{}^{\kappa}\kappa\to{}^{\kappa}\kappa\mid\forall b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa(\tau(b)<b)\right\}
, and define a relation
J_{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{0}\times\mathcal{X}_{1}
by
(f,b)\mathrel{J}_{\infty}\tau
iff
f=^{\infty}\tau(b)
. We use this to define the relational system
\mathscr{J}_{\infty}
, which is equivalent to the categorical product
\bigotimes_{b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa}\mathscr{E}\!\mathscr{D}_{b}^{\bot}
. We also define a relational system whose norms are the covering and uniformity numbers of the
\kappa
-strong measure zero ideal.

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{X}_{1},J_{\infty}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{J}_{\infty}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{J}_{\infty}^{\bot}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}\!\!\mathscr{N}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{}^{\kappa}2,\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa},\in\right\rangle

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{S}\!\!\mathscr{N}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathscr{S}\!\!\mathscr{N}^{\bot}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})

The following Tukey connection proves
\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\geq\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})
and
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})
.

Theorem 4.11

\mathscr{S}\!\!\mathscr{N}\succeq\mathscr{J}_{\infty}
.

We describe
\rho_{-}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\to{}^{\kappa}2
and
\rho_{+}:\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}\to\mathcal{X}_{1}
.

For each
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
let
\beta^{b}_{\alpha}\in\kappa
be minimal such that there exists an injection
\iota^{b}_{\alpha}:b(\alpha)\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow}{}^{\beta^{b}_{\alpha}}2
and let
\gamma^{b}_{\alpha}
be the ordinal sum
\sum_{\xi<\alpha}\beta^{b}_{\xi}
.

For any
(f,b)\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
, we define
\rho_{-}(f,b)=f^{\prime}\in{}^{\kappa}2
piecewise by:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\restriction[\gamma^{b}_{\alpha},\gamma^{b}_{\alpha+1}):\gamma^{b}_{\alpha}+\xi\mapsto\iota^{b}_{\alpha}(f(\alpha))(\xi).

Given
X\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
and
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, we can find
\overline{s}^{b}=\langle s^{b}_{\alpha}\in{}^{\gamma^{b}_{\alpha+1}}2\mid\alpha\in\kappa\rangle
such that
X
is cofinally covered by
\overline{s}^{b}
. Define
t^{b}_{\alpha}\in{}^{\beta_{\alpha}^{b}}2
by
t^{b}_{\alpha}:\xi\mapsto s^{b}_{\alpha}(\gamma_{\alpha}^{b}+\xi)
. Let
\rho_{+}(X)=\tau
, where
\tau(b):\alpha\mapsto(\iota_{\alpha}^{b})^{-1}(t^{b}_{\alpha})
if this is defined and arbitrary otherwise.

Given
(f,b)\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
with
\rho_{-}(f,b)=f^{\prime}
and
X\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
with
\rho_{+}(X)=\tau
, suppose that
f^{\prime}\in X
, then since
X
is cofinally covered by
\overline{s}^{b}
, there are cofinally many
\alpha
such that
f^{\prime}\in[s_{\alpha}^{b}]
, hence for such
\alpha
we have
f^{\prime}\restriction[\gamma_{\alpha}^{b},\gamma_{\alpha+1}^{b})=s^{b}_{\alpha}\restriction[\gamma_{\alpha}^{b},\gamma_{\alpha+1}^{b})
. But then
t_{\alpha}^{b}=\iota_{\alpha}^{b}(f(\alpha))
, and thus
\tau(b)(\alpha)=f(\alpha)
. Thus
f=^{\infty}\tau(b)
, or equivalently
(f,b)\mathrel{J}_{\infty}\tau
.
∎

As said,
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})
is actually equal to
\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
. We prove the remaining direction below.

Theorem 4.12

\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})\geq\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
.

Let
\pi:{}^{<\kappa}2\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow}\kappa
be some fixed bijection and
X\notin\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
, then there exists
f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
X
is not cofinally covered for any
\overline{s}=\left\langle s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
with
s_{\alpha}\in{}^{f(\alpha)}2
. For each
x\in X
let
g_{x}:\alpha\mapsto\pi(x\restriction f(\alpha))
and define
b:\alpha\mapsto\sup\left\{\pi(s)+1\mid s\in{}^{f(\alpha)}2\right\}
, then
g_{x}\in\prod b
. We set
D=\left\{g_{x}\mid x\in X\right\}
.

Given
h\in\prod b
, for each
\alpha
define
s_{\alpha}=\pi^{-1}(h(\alpha))
if
\mathrm{dom}(\pi^{-1}(h(\alpha))=f(\alpha)
and
s_{\alpha}\in{}^{f(\alpha)}2
arbitrary otherwise. If
h(\alpha)=g_{x}(\alpha)
, then
s_{\alpha}=x\restriction f(\alpha)
, hence
x\in[s_{\alpha}]
. Since
X
is not cofinally covered by
\overline{s}=\left\langle s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
, there exists
x\in X
such that
h\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}g_{x}
. Hence
D
forms a witness for
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
.
∎

We cannot prove the dual claim that
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})\leq\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
, since
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and it is consistent that
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})\leq\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})<\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})
. The model for this is a generalisation of the Sacks model. We give a sketch of the argument below. We will use the generalised Sacks forcing from
[
Kan80
]
, which is equivalent to the forcing notion
\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
from
5.11
with
b=h=\overline{2}
. We refer to that section for definitions.

Theorem 4.13

Let
\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
be the generalised Sacks forcing and let
\overline{\mathbb{S}}
be the
{\leq}\kappa
-support iteration of
\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
of length
\kappa^{++}
. If
\mathbf{V}\vDash\text{``}\,2^{\kappa}=\kappa^{+}\,\text{''}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}\vDash\text{``}\,\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\kappa^{+}<\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})=\kappa^{++}\,\text{''}.

It is important to note that
\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
is
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
-bounding, see
[
Kan80
]
. Because of this, there exists a
\subseteq
-cofinal subset of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
whose elements can be coded terms of a family of sequences indexed by some fixed dominating family. Since the dominating family remains dominating, we can essentially characterise the elements of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
from the ground model. Subsequently, we show that
\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
adds a generic
\kappa
-real that is not an element of any
X\in(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})^{\mathbf{V}}
. This concludes the proof, since any
\kappa^{+}
-sized witness of
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa})
obtained in an intermediate step of the iteration is destroyed by the subsequent step, and it is known that
{\leq}\kappa
-support iteration of
\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
does not increase
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
(see e.g.
[
BBTFM18
]
).

Since
\mathbf{V}\vDash\text{``}\,2^{\kappa}=\kappa^{+}\,\text{''}
, we may fix some dominating family
D\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
of size
\kappa^{+}
. Let
\sigma=\left\{\overline{s}^{f}\mid f\in D\right\}
be a family of sequences, where
\overline{s}^{f}=\langle s_{\xi}^{f}\mid\xi\in\kappa\rangle
is such that
s_{\xi}^{f}\in{}^{f(\xi)}2
. We use
\sigma
to define an element of
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket=\textstyle\bigcap_{f\in D}\bigcap_{\alpha_{0}\in\kappa}\bigcup_{\xi\in[\alpha_{0},\kappa)}[s_{\xi}^{f}]\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}

Reversely it’s easy to see that for every
X\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{\kappa}
there exists some choice of
\sigma
such that
X\subseteq\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket
. We show that the set of conditions
S\in\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
with
[S]\cap\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket=\varnothing
is dense. It suffices to show that for each
S\in\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
there exists
f\in D
and
T\leq S
such that
[T]\cap\bigcup_{\xi\in\kappa}[s_{\xi}^{f}]=\varnothing
.

Let
S\in\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
be arbitrary. We may assume without loss of generality that for each
\alpha
there is
\beta_{\alpha}
such that
\mathrm{Split}_{\alpha}(S)\subseteq{}^{\beta_{\alpha}}2
, and thus that
\left\langle\beta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
enumerates the club set of the heights of splitting levels of
S
. Now let
\gamma_{\xi}
denote the
\xi
-th limit ordinal below
\kappa
and consider the nonstationary set
\left\{\beta_{\gamma_{\xi}+1}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\}
. Let
f_{0}:\xi\mapsto\beta_{\gamma_{\xi}+1}+1
and pick
f\in D
such that
f_{0}\leq^{*}f
.

For each
\xi\in\kappa
such that
f_{0}(\xi)\leq f(\xi)
and
s^{f}_{\xi}\restriction\beta_{\gamma_{\xi}+1}\in S
, we prune
S
by removing the part of the tree generated by the successor
s^{f}_{\xi}\restriction(\beta_{\gamma_{\xi}+1}+1)
to get a tree
T\leq S
. Since
f_{0}\leq^{*}f
, it follows that
[T]\cap\left\llbracket\sigma\right\rrbracket=\varnothing
. Finally, we only prune
S
at successor splitting levels, thus
T\in\mathbb{S}_{\kappa}
.
∎

We will prove the connection between
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and
\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
, and between
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
directly, as it will be more similar to the proof of
4.16
.

Lemma 4.14

\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}
and

\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}
.

Remember that by
2.11
we have
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
, hence by
2.10

\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}
. Moreover, it is clear from
3.34
that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
.

Secondly, we prove that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
implies
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
. Let
F\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be a witness for
|F|=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and assume
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
. Since
|F|<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
, there exists
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
f<^{*}b
for all
f\in F
. Let
f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto f(\alpha)
if
f(\alpha)\in b(\alpha)
and
f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto 0
otherwise. Clearly
f^{\prime}\in\prod b
and for any
g\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
we have
f=^{\infty}g
iff
f^{\prime}=^{\infty}g
. Therefore
F^{\prime}=\left\{f^{\prime}\mid f\in F\right\}\subseteq\prod b
is a witness for
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
, thus we see:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=|F|\geq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\geq\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\geq\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}).

For the dual result, assume
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
and towards contradiction let
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
as well. Let
D\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
witness
|D|=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
and for each
b\in D
choose a witness
F_{b}\subseteq\prod b
for
|F_{b}|=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\leq\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
. Define
F=\bigcup_{b\in D}F_{b}
, then
|F|=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})\cdot\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
, thus there exists
g\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
g\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}f
for all
f\in F
. Let
b\in D
be such that
g<^{*}b
and let
g^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto g(\alpha)
if
g(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
and
g^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto 0
otherwise, then
g^{\prime}\in\prod b
and
g^{\prime}\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}f
for all
f\in F_{b}
. But this contradicts that
F_{b}
witnesses
|F_{b}|=\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
.

Putting everything together, we have showed that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})\right\}.

We will now show a generalisation of
4.7
. It is not possible to prove the generalisation for every
b,h,h^{\prime}\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
with
h\leq h^{\prime}
, since we will see in the next section that it is consistent that
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,2^{h}}(\in^{*})=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},2^{h}}(\in^{*})<\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h}(\in^{*})
for all
b^{\prime}\geq b
.

Lemma 4.15

For any
b,h,h^{\prime}\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that there exists a continuous strictly increasing sequence
\left\langle\beta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
with
h^{\prime}(\alpha)\leq h(\xi)
for all
\xi\geq\beta_{\alpha}
, there exists
b^{\prime}\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
\mathscr{L}_{b,h}\preceq\mathscr{L}_{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}
.

Let
I_{\alpha}=[\beta_{\alpha},\beta_{\alpha+1})
for each
\alpha\in\kappa
, where we assume without loss of generality that
\beta_{0}=0
. Since
\left\langle\beta_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
is continuous, we see that
\left\langle I_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
is an interval partition of
\kappa
. We define
b^{\prime}(\alpha)=\left|\prod_{\xi\in I_{\alpha}}b(\xi)\right|
and a bijection
\pi_{\alpha}:b^{\prime}(\alpha)\mathrel{\textnormal{›}\mathrel{\mkern-9.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow}\prod_{\xi\in I_{\alpha}}b(\xi)
for each
\alpha\in\kappa
.

\rho_{-}:\prod b\to\prod b^{\prime}
is defined by
\rho_{-}(f)=f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto\pi_{\alpha}^{-1}(f\restriction I_{\alpha})
. We define
\rho_{+}:\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}\to\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
by
\rho_{+}(\varphi^{\prime})=\varphi:\xi\mapsto\left\{\pi_{\alpha}(x)(\xi)\mid x\in\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)\right\}
, where
\alpha
is such that
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
. Note that this is well-defined, since
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
implies
\xi\geq\beta_{\alpha}
, and thus
|\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)|<h^{\prime}(\alpha)\leq h(\xi)
.

Suppose that
f\in\prod b
,
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b^{\prime},h^{\prime}}
and
\rho_{-}(f)=f^{\prime}
,
\rho_{+}(\varphi^{\prime})=\varphi
. Let
\alpha\in\kappa
be such that
f^{\prime}(\alpha)\in\varphi^{\prime}(\alpha)
. For any
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
, we then see that
\pi_{\alpha}(f^{\prime}(\alpha))(\xi)=f(\xi)
, thus
f(\xi)\in\varphi(\xi)
for all
\xi\in I_{\alpha}
. Hence, if
f^{\prime}\in^{*}\varphi^{\prime}
, it follows that
f\in^{*}\varphi
.
∎

Classically, it can be shown analogously to
4.14
that
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{N})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\omega}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{inf}_{\omega}(\in^{*})\right\}
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{N})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\omega}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{sup}_{\omega}(\in^{*})\right\}
. There is a generalisation of this result for
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
as well, although we have to replace
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{N})
by their combinatorial counterparts
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
(nota bene: these are the cardinals from the (un)bounded generalised Baire space
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, or equivalently with the bound
b=\overline{\kappa}
). Moreover, since the parameter
h
is important, in the sense that differing
h
leads to different cardinals, we can only prove a parametrised version of this result.

Lemma 4.16

(cf.
[
CM19
]
Lemma 3.12)

\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})=\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\right\}
and

\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})=\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\right\}
for any
h\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

That
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\leq\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\right\}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\geq\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\right\}
are clear.

Let
F\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
with
|F|<\min\left\{\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\right\}
, then there exists
b\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
such that
f<^{*}b
for all
f\in F
. Let
F^{\prime}=\left\{f^{\prime}\mid f\in F\right\}
where
f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto f(\alpha)
if
f(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
and
f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto 0
otherwise, then
f=^{*}f^{\prime}\in\prod b
. Finally, there exists
\varphi\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
such that
f^{\prime}\in^{*}\varphi
for all
f^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
by
|F^{\prime}|<\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\leq\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
. Then also
f\in^{*}\varphi
for all
f\in F
, thus
|F|<\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
.

Let
D\subseteq{}^{\kappa}\kappa
be a witness for
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
with
|D|=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
. For each
b\in D
choose a witness
\Phi_{b}\subseteq\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
for
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
with
|\Phi_{b}|=\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
. Let
\Phi=\bigcup_{b\in D}\Phi_{b}
, then
|\Phi|\leq\max\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*}),\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})\right\}
. If
f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, then there is
b\in D
such that
f<^{*}b
. Again, let
f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto f(\alpha)
if
f(\alpha)<b(\alpha)
and
f^{\prime}:\alpha\mapsto 0
otherwise, then
f^{\prime}\in\prod b
. Therefore, there exists
\varphi\in\Phi_{b}
such that
f^{\prime}\in^{*}\varphi
, and thus such that
f\in^{*}\varphi
. This shows that
\Phi
is a witness for
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
.
∎

5. Consistency of Strict Inequalities

In this section we will discuss consistency of strict relations between cardinals we have discussed so far. We will first discuss the
\kappa
-Cohen model and the
\kappa
-Hechler model. Afterwards we show how we can separate cardinals of the form
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
and of the form
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
for different parameters
b,h
. In the first case, we can find
\kappa^{+}
many parameters
\left\langle b_{\xi},h_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa^{+}\right\rangle
and a forcing extension in which the cardinals
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b_{\xi},h_{\xi}}(\in^{*})
are mutually distinct. In the second case, we can describe
\kappa
many parameters
\left\langle b_{\xi},h_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\kappa\right\rangle
such that for any finite
A\subseteq\kappa
there exists a forcing extension in which the cardinals
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b_{\xi},h_{\xi}}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
with
\xi\in A
are mutually distinct.

Before we continue, let us establish some notation regarding trees and product forcing.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a set of functions such that
\mathrm{dom}(f)=\kappa
for each
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{<\kappa}=\left\{f\restriction\alpha\mid f\in\mathcal{F}\land\alpha\in\kappa\right\}
be the set of initial segments of functions in
\mathcal{F}
. Notably, if
\mathcal{F}=\prod b
, then
\mathcal{F}_{<\kappa}=\prod_{<\kappa}b
and if
\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{Loc}_{\kappa}^{b,h}
, then
\mathcal{F}_{<\kappa}=\mathrm{Loc}_{<\kappa}^{b,h}
.

A subset
T\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{<\kappa}
is called a
tree on
\mathcal{F}
if for every
u\in T
and
\beta\in\mathrm{dom}(u)
we have
u\restriction\beta\in T
. A subset
C\subseteq T
is a
chain
if for any
u,v\in C
we have
u\subseteq v
or
v\subseteq u
, and
C
is called
maximal
if there exists no chain
C^{\prime}\subseteq T
with
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}
. A function
b:\alpha\to\kappa
where
\alpha\leq\kappa
is called a
branch
of
T
if there exists a maximal chain
C\subseteq T
such that
b=\bigcup C
. The set of branches of
T
is denoted by
[T]
. We define the
subtree
of
T
generated by
u\in T
as:

\displaystyle(T)_{u}=\left\{v\in T\mid u\subseteq v\lor v\subseteq u\right\}

Given
u\in\mathcal{F}_{<\kappa}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
such that
u\subseteq f
, let
x=f(\mathrm{dom}(u))
, then we write
u^{\frown}\left\langle x\right\rangle
for
f\restriction(\mathrm{dom}(u)+1)
. If
u\in T
, let
v\in T
be a
successor
of
u
if there exists
x
such that
v=u^{\frown}\left\langle x\right\rangle
. We denote the set of successors of
u
in
T
by
\mathrm{suc}(u,T)
.

We call
u
a
\lambda
-splitting
node (of
T
), if
\lambda\leq\left|\mathrm{suc}(u,T)\right|
. We say
u
is a
splitting
node if it is a 2-splitting node, and a
non-splitting
node otherwise. If
u
is a
\lambda
-splitting node, but not a
\mu
-splitting node for any cardinal
\mu
with
\lambda<\mu
, then we say that
u
is a
sharp

\lambda
-splitting node. We let
\mathrm{Split}_{\alpha}(T)
be the set of all
u\in T
such that
u
is splitting and

\displaystyle\mathrm{ot}(\left\{\beta\in\mathrm{dom}(u)\mid u\restriction\beta\text{ is splitting}\right\})=\alpha

where
\mathrm{ot}(X)
denotes the order-type of
(X,\in)
. We let
\mathrm{Lev}_{\xi}(T)=\left\{u\in T\mid\mathrm{dom}(u)=\xi\right\}
denote the
\xi
-th level of the tree
T
.

Given a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
, then a sequence
\left\langle\leq_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
on
\mathbb{P}
is called a
fusion ordering
when:

\bullet

q\leq_{0}p
iff
q\leq p
, and

\bullet

q\leq_{\beta}p
implies
q\leq_{\alpha}p
for all
\alpha<\beta
.

A
fusion sequence
is a sequence of conditions
\left\langle p_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
such that
p_{\beta}\leq_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\leq\beta\in\kappa
. We say that
\mathbb{P}
is
closed under fusion
if every fusion sequence
\left\langle p_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
has some
p
with
p\leq_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a set of ordinals and
\mathbb{P}_{\xi}
be a forcing notion for each
\xi\in\mathcal{A}
. We will denote
\leq_{\mathbb{P}_{\xi}}
as
\leq^{\xi}
, or more commonly as
\leq
when there is no possibility for confusion, and we denote
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{P}_{\xi}}
as
\mathbbm{1}_{\xi}
. Consider the set
\mathcal{C}
of (choice) functions
p:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{\bigcup}_{\xi\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathbb{P}_{\xi}
such that
p(\xi)\in\mathbb{P}_{\xi}
for each
\xi\in{\mathcal{A}}
. For any
p\in\mathcal{C}
, we define the
support
of
p
as
\mathrm{supp}(p)=\{\xi\in{\mathcal{A}}\mid p(\xi)\neq\mathbbm{1}_{\xi}\}
. We define the
{\leq}\kappa
-support product as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{P}}=\textstyle\prod^{\leq\kappa}_{\xi\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mathbb{P}_{\xi}=\left\{p\in\mathcal{C}\ \middle|\ \left|\mathrm{supp}(p)\right|\leq\kappa\right\}.

If
p,q\in\overline{\mathbb{P}}
, then
q\leq_{\overline{\mathbb{P}}}p
iff
q(\xi)\leq^{\xi}p(\xi)
for all
\xi\in{\mathcal{A}}
. We will again write
q\leq p
instead of
q\leq_{\overline{\mathbb{P}}}p
if the context is clear, and we will write
\overline{\mathbbm{1}}
for
\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{\mathbb{P}}}
.

If
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
is a product of forcing notions
\mathbb{P}_{\xi}
such that each
\mathbb{P}_{\xi}
has a fusion ordering
\leq_{\alpha}^{\xi}
, then we define for each
p,q\in\overline{\mathbb{P}}
,
\alpha\in\kappa
, and
Z\subseteq\mathcal{A}
with
|Z|<\kappa
a
generalised fusion relation

q\leq_{Z,\alpha}p
iff
q\leq_{\overline{\mathbb{P}}}p
and for each
\xi\in Z
we have
q(\xi)\leq_{\alpha}^{\xi}p(\xi)
.

A
generalised fusion sequence
is a sequence
\left\langle(p_{\alpha},Z_{\alpha})\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
such that:

1.

p_{\alpha}\in\overline{\mathbb{P}}
and
Z_{\alpha}\in[\mathcal{A}]^{<\kappa}
for each
\alpha\in\kappa
,

2.

p_{\beta}\leq_{Z_{\alpha},\alpha}p_{\alpha}
and
Z_{\alpha}\subseteq Z_{\beta}
for all
\alpha\leq\beta\in\kappa
,

3.

for limit
\delta
we have
Z_{\delta}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\delta}Z_{\alpha}
,

4.

\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}Z_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}\mathrm{supp}(p_{\alpha})
.

We say that
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
is
closed under generalised fusion
if every generalised fusion sequence
\left\langle(p_{\alpha},Z_{\alpha})\mid\alpha\in\kappa\right\rangle
has some
p\in\overline{\mathbb{P}}
with
\mathrm{supp}(p)=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\kappa}\mathrm{supp}(p)
and
p\leq_{\alpha,Z_{\alpha}}p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\kappa
. Note that point 4. implies that for each
\xi\in\mathcal{A}
we have
p(\xi)\leq_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}(\xi)
for almost all
\alpha\in\kappa
.

Suppose
X\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{P}}
and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, we define

\displaystyle X\restriction\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\left\{p\restriction\mathcal{B}\mid p\in X\right\}

Let
\mathcal{B}^{c}=\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{B}
and
G\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{P}}
be
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
-generic over
\mathbf{V}
, then clearly
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
and
(\overline{\mathbb{P}}\restriction\mathcal{B})\times(\overline{\mathbb{P}}\restriction\mathcal{B}^{c})
are forcing equivalent,
(G\restriction\mathcal{B})\times(G\restriction\mathcal{B}^{c})
is
(\overline{\mathbb{P}}\restriction\mathcal{B})\times(\overline{\mathbb{P}}\restriction\mathcal{B}^{c})
-generic and

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}[G]=\mathbf{V}[(G\restriction\mathcal{B})\times(G\restriction\mathcal{B}^{c})]=\mathbf{V}[G\restriction\mathcal{B}][G\restriction\mathcal{B}^{c}].

5.1. The
\kappa
-Cohen model

We will first define
\kappa
-Cohen forcing.

Definition 5.1

Let
\lambda\geq\kappa
be a cardinal. We define
\kappa
-Cohen forcing
adding
\lambda
many
\kappa
-Cohen reals as the set
\mathbb{C}_{\kappa}^{\lambda}
with as conditions functions
p
with
\mathrm{dom}(p)\in[\lambda\times\kappa]^{<\kappa}
and values
\mathrm{ran}(p)\subseteq\kappa
ordered by
q\leq p
iff
q\supseteq p
.

The effect of
\kappa
-Cohen forcing is analogous to the effect of Cohen forcing in the context of
{}^{\omega}\omega
. Namely,
\kappa
-Cohen reals lie outside of any
\kappa
-meagre set coded in the ground model, and any
\kappa^{+}
many
\kappa
-Cohen reals form a
\kappa
-nonmeagre set. Consequently, if
\mathrm{cf}(\lambda)>\kappa
, then after forcing with
\mathbb{C}_{\kappa}^{\lambda}
we have
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\lambda=2^{\kappa}
, while
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\kappa^{+}
. Particularly,
\kappa^{+}=\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})<\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\kappa^{++}
holds in the
\kappa
-Cohen model
, which we will define as the result of forcing with
\mathbb{C}_{\kappa}^{\kappa^{++}}
over
\mathbf{V}\vDash\text{``}\,\mathsf{GCH}\,\text{''}
. We will not give a proof of these facts here, as they are well-known and could be found, for example, as Proposition 47 in
[
BBTFM18
]
.

The effect this has on the bounded cardinals we have discussed so far, is that the
\mathfrak{d}
-side cardinals are all large in the
\kappa
-Cohen model, since
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
is a lower bound for these cardinals, whereas the
\mathfrak{b}
-side cardinals are all small, since
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
is an upper bound. That is, the diagram from
Figure

1
is divided as follows:

\kappa^{+}
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\kappa^{++}

Figure 2:
The
\kappa
-Cohen model

5.2. The
\kappa
-Hechler models

We have already encountered generalised Hechler forcing in
3.8
as the forcing notion
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{b}(C)
, where
b
is a function with domain
\kappa
and
C\subseteq\kappa
is club. The most natural form of this forcing notion is
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}\equiv\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{\overline{\kappa}}(\kappa)
. We will restate the definition for
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
individually below.

Definition 5.2

We define
\kappa
-Hechler forcing
as the forcing notion
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
with conditions
(s,f)
such that
s\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa
and
f\in{}^{\kappa}\kappa
. The ordering is defined by
(t,g)\leq(s,f)
iff
s\subseteq t
and
f\leq g
and for every
\alpha\in\mathrm{dom}(t)\setminus\mathrm{dom}(s)
we have
f(\alpha)\leq t(\alpha)
.

As with
\kappa
-Cohen forcing, the effect of
\kappa
-Hechler forcing is in many aspects similar to the situation on
{}^{\omega}\omega
. For instance,
\kappa
-Hechler forcing adds a dominating
\kappa
-real and a
\kappa
-Cohen real, is
{<}\kappa
-closed,
\kappa
-centred (which we define below), and thus has the
{<}\kappa^{+}
-c.c.. As a consequence of this, a
{<}\kappa
-support iteration of
\kappa
-Hechler forcing of length
\kappa^{++}
over
\mathbf{V}\vDash\text{``}\,2^{\kappa}=\kappa^{+}\,\text{''}
will increase both
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})
and
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
, and thus by
2.10
it will result in a model where
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\kappa^{++}
. We will call this model the
\kappa
-Hechler model
. Classically, Hechler forcing does not add random reals, and therefore
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{N})
and
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{N})
stay small in the Hechler model. A similar thing happens in the generalised case, where the analogue for
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{N})
, namely
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
, remains of size
\kappa^{+}
in the
\kappa
-Hechler model. We will show in this section that all of the cardinal characteristics on bounded spaces on the
\mathfrak{b}
-side will remain small as well. Specifically
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\kappa^{+}
in the
\kappa
-Hechler model.

\kappa^{+}
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\kappa^{++}

Figure 3:
The
\kappa
-Hechler model

The above diagram summarises the situation in the
\kappa
-Hechler model, where the diagram is adapted from
Figure

1
with the addition of
\mathfrak{sup}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
, and with
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
replaced by the smaller
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
.

A closely related model to the
\kappa
-Hechler model, is the
dual
\kappa
-Hechler model
, which we will define as the result of a
{<}\kappa
-support iteration of
\kappa
-Hechler forcing of length (the ordinal)
\kappa^{++}\cdot\kappa^{+}
over
\mathbf{V}\vDash\text{``}\,\mathsf{GCH}\,\text{''}
. In this case, a cofinal set of
\kappa
-Hechler reals of size
\kappa^{+}
will form a dominating family, and since they are
\kappa
-Cohen generic as well, they form a witness for a
\kappa
-nonmeagre set of size
\kappa^{+}
. Therefore, in the dual
\kappa
-Hechler model we have
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}(\leq^{*})=\mathrm{non}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\kappa^{+}
, and by
2.10
also
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})=\kappa^{+}
. For similar reasons as before, the
\kappa
-Hechler forcing does not affect the size of the cardinal characteristics on bounded spaces, and thus we will show that the cardinals on the
\mathfrak{d}
-side remain large.

We can summarise the dual
\kappa
-Hechler model as follows:

\kappa^{+}
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\leq^{*})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\mathrel{\cancel{\ni^{\infty}}})
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b,h}(\in^{*})
\mathfrak{inf}_{\kappa}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})
\mathrm{cov}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
\kappa^{++}

Figure 4:
The dual
\kappa
-Hechler model

We refer to section 4 of
[
BBTFM18
]
for proofs of the sizes of
\mathrm{add}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and
\mathrm{cof}(\mathcal{M}_{\kappa})
and of
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{h}(\in^{*})
in the two models and focus here on the cardinalities of the cardinals defined on bounded spaces.

It will be sufficient to prove that
\mathfrak{b}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\kappa^{+}
in the
\kappa
-Hechler model and
\mathfrak{d}_{\kappa}^{b}(\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}})=\kappa^{++}
in the dual
\kappa
-Hechler model and thus we focus on the
\mathrel{\cancel{=^{\infty}}}
relation in particular. The main lemma we need concerns
\kappa
-centred forcing notions, which is a generalisation of
\sigma
-centredness to the context of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
.

Definition 5.3

Let
\mathbb{P}
be a forcing notion. In the context of
{}^{\kappa}\kappa
, we will define a subset
P\subseteq\mathbb{P}
to be
{<}\kappa
-linked
if for every subset
Q\in[P]^{<\kappa}
there exists
r\in\mathbb{P}
such that
r\leq q
for all
q\in Q
. We say that
\mathbb{P}
is
\kappa
-centred
if
\mathbb{P}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\kappa}\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
where each
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
is
{<}\kappa
-linked.

Lemma 5.4

Let
\mathbb{P}
be a
\kappa
-centred forcing notion and
\dot{f}
be a
\mathbb{P}
-name such that
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\text{``}\,\dot{f}\in\prod b\,\text{''}
, then there is a family
\left\{g_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\kappa\right\}\subseteq\prod b
in the ground model such that for any
h\in\prod b
with
h=^{\infty}g_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\kappa
we have
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\text{``}\,h=^{\infty}\dot{f}\,\text{''}
. Consequently, there exists
g\in\prod b
such that
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\text{``}\,g=^{\infty}\dot{f}\,\text{''}
.

We let
\mathbb{P}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\kappa}\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
such that each
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
is
{<}\kappa
-linked and we let
\dot{f}
be a
\mathbb{P}
-name such that
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\text{``}\,\dot{f}\in\prod b\,\text{''}
. Let
\alpha,\gamma\in\kappa
, then there exists
\beta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\in b(\alpha)
such that for all
p\in\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
we have
p\mathrel{\cancel{\Vdash}}\text{``}\,\dot{f}(\alpha)\neq\beta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\,\text{''}
: if not, let
p_{\beta}\in\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
be such that
p_{\beta}\Vdash\text{``}\,\dot{f}(\alpha)\neq\beta\,\text{''}
, then
\left\{p_{\beta}\mid\beta\in b(\alpha)\right\}
has no common extension, contradicting that
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
is
{<}\kappa
-linked. Let
g_{\gamma}:\alpha\mapsto\beta^{\gamma}_{\alpha}
.

Suppose that
h\in\prod b
and
h=^{\infty}g_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\kappa
and let
\alpha_{0}\in\kappa
and
p\in\mathbb{P}
be arbitrary. There exists
\gamma\in\kappa
such that
p\in\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
, and since
h=^{\infty}g_{\gamma}
we can find
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
such that
h(\alpha)=g_{\gamma}(\alpha)=\beta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}
. But, by construction of
\beta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}
we know that
p\mathrel{\cancel{\Vdash}}\text{``}\,\dot{f}(\alpha)\neq\beta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\,\text{''}
, thus there exists
p^{\prime}\leq p
such that
p^{\prime}\Vdash\text{``}\,\dot{f}(\alpha)=\beta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}=h(\alpha)\,\text{''}
. Since
\alpha_{0}
and
p
were arbitrary, we see that
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\text{``}\,\dot{f}=^{\infty}h\,\text{''}
.

To find
g\in\prod b
such that
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\text{``}\,g=^{\infty}\dot{f}\,\text{''}
, fix some surjection
\pi:\kappa\rightarrow\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow\kappa
such that
|\pi^{-1}(\gamma)|=\kappa
for all
\gamma\in\kappa
and let
g:\alpha\mapsto\beta_{\alpha}^{\pi(\alpha)}
.
∎

It is not difficult to see that
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
is
\kappa
-centred, and thus that the above lemma applies to
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
. However, we will use an iteration of
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
and it is not the case that every iteration of
\kappa
-centred forcing notions is itself
\kappa
-centred. However, for short iterations (of length
{<}(2^{\kappa})^{+}
) of forcing notions where we can give a canonical bound in the ground model, we can show that
\kappa
-centredness is preserved. We will make this precise with the following definition and lemma, although we will refer to Lemma 55 of
[
BBTFM18
]
for a proof of the lemma.

Definition 5.5

A forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
is
\kappa
-centred with canonical bounds
if
\mathbb{P}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\kappa}\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
where each
\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
is
{<}\kappa
-linked,
\mathbb{P}
is
{<}\kappa
-closed and there exists
f^{\mathbb{P}}:{}^{<\kappa}\kappa\to\kappa
such that for every
\lambda<\kappa
and decreasing sequence
\left\langle p_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\lambda\right\rangle
with
p_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{P}_{\gamma_{\alpha}}
there exists
p\in\mathbb{P}_{\gamma}
with
\gamma=f^{\mathbb{P}}(\left\langle\gamma_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\lambda\right\rangle)
such that
p\leq p_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\lambda
.

Lemma 5.6

([BBTFM18] Lemma 55)

(Let
\kappa
be inaccessible.) Let
\mu<(2^{\kappa})^{+}
and
\langle\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\mu\rangle
be a
{<}\kappa
-support iteration, where each
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}
is the name of a forcing notion that is
\kappa
-centred with canonical bounds given by a function
f^{\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{\alpha}}\in\mathbf{V}
. Then
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
is
\kappa
-centred.

Naturally,
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
is
\kappa
-centred with canonical bounds.

Lemma 5.7

\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
is
\kappa
-centred with canonical bounds.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{s}=\left\{s\right\}\times{}^{\kappa}\kappa\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
, then
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{s}
is clearly
{<}\kappa
-linked and
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}=\bigcup_{s\in{}^{<\kappa}\kappa}\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{s}
is therefore
\kappa
-centred. It is also easy to see that
\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}
is
{<}\kappa
-closed, since for any decreasing sequence
\left\langle(s_{\alpha},g_{\alpha})\mid\alpha\in\lambda\right\rangle
with
\lambda<\kappa
we can find a function
g
such that
g\geq g_{\alpha}
